ordinary required `mode`, and later composer state uses the existing mode endpoint. Creating the
shared chat never copies an unrelated conversation's selection or elevated permissions from its opener.

In team mode, all permitted readers see the same conversation. Every human message carries its
authenticated sender in `metadata.userId`, exactly as task chats do; clients cannot substitute
an owner in the body. Trusted sender context changes with each accepted human message, so the
agent works for that sender rather than the person who first opened the chat or created the
Surface. Standalone messages use the existing null/absent local-user convention. Reference
chat reads, messages, agent snapshots, and events also obey the target's existing read access;
Surface membership or a known chat ID never bypasses it. A caller lacking that access receives
`404 not_found`. Target lifecycle state alone does not erase the chat or its history.

The Surface owns one plain, non-Git folder at `<public Happy folder>/Surfaces/<surfaceId>`,
beside the `Tasks` and `Bots` roots. As with task creation, configured runners place it on the
default runner using that runner's platform layout (`~/Happy/Surfaces/<surfaceId>` on macOS,
`~/happy/Surfaces/<surfaceId>` otherwise); without runners it uses the local workload/public
Happy folder. Configuration owns path resolution. The folder and all three IDs stay fixed.
Its dedicated workspace has `kind: "surface"`, optional additive `surfaceId`, `botId: null`,
no `taskId`, `parentId: null`, `projectId: null`, `base: null`, and exactly this primary agent.
It is discovered through the chat agent's `workspaceId`, not project/workspace listings or
bootstrap workspace arrays. Its normal file, terminal, and proxy operations work by
workspace ID under the same permission model as task workspaces.

The Surface owns the lifetime. Agent archive, unarchive, and reorder; workspace rename,
archive, reorder, and child creation; and attaching another root agent all return `409 conflict`.
Surface membership changes never stop the chat, delete its folder, or discard history, even
when nobody remains joined. Since Surfaces have no deletion route, their chats and folders
remain for the Surface's lifetime. The agent's context names its Surface ID, type, and stored
label, and tells it to use ordinary Surface tools, plus document tools for document content,
calendar tools for calendar events, and the read-only message tools for a chat Surface's
conversation. A calendar chat's context calls its Surface a calendar. A chat Surface's agent
context calls its Surface a conversation among people, and tells the agent it reads that
conversation but cannot post to it, so it answers in its own shared chat.
It receives no admin role, no new subtask-creation eligibility, and no implicit join, creation,
or target permissions. Ensuring or using the chat never joins anyone to a Surface or task list.

## Tasks

A task is a bot-like continuous chat for one piece of work. Like a bot it is created with exactly
one agent, keeps that agent for its whole life, and owns one dedicated folder. Unlike a bot it has
no avatar and no administration, and it records who it belongs to: the person whose message led
to its creation. A task is the root of its own subtasks.

A person creates a task through `POST /v0/tasks`. An agent creates one through its `create_task`
tool on behalf of the person it is working for: an active bot's agent, or another user-controlled
root. The agent that created a task may message and archive it through its task tools; people
rename, archive, and unarchive tasks through the routes below. Each task's folder lives under the
daemon's tasks root beside the bots root, `~/Happy/Tasks/<folderName>`, or on the default runner
while runners are configured.

**Owner.** `ownerUserId` is the installation-local Happy user ID of the person the task belongs
to, resolvable through `GET /v0/users` like message `metadata.userId`. For a task created through
`POST /v0/tasks` it is the authenticated team member who created it. For a task an agent created
it is the team member whose message the creating agent was working on, and it is `null` when no
person is identified, for example when another agent's message led to the task. It is `null` in
standalone mode. It never changes after creation.

**Memberships and per-person order.** Any authenticated person may join any task, archived ones
included. Joining puts the task in that person's own task list; leaving takes it out. Each
person's list has its own order, held by their membership, so reordering moves a task only in the
caller's list and never changes another person's list or the task itself. The owner joins when
the task is created, at the top of their list; a newly joined task also goes to the top. The owner
may leave like anyone else and remains the owner. In standalone mode the installation's one
person is the only member; in team mode a membership belongs to the authenticated team member.
Leaving and archiving are separate: leaving never archives a task, even when its owner or its last
member leaves, and archiving never removes a membership, so an archived task stays in every list
that holds it.

**Automatic membership.** Every team member automatically joins all active tasks by default,
including tasks created by other people or agents and tasks that already exist when the member
finishes their profile. Guests of exported bots are excluded. Existing members acquire missing
active-task memberships after daemon startup. Automatic joins use ordinary memberships and their
private `task.joined` events, place newly joined tasks at the top, and preserve the order of tasks
already joined. They do not change ownership, archival permission, or subtask visibility.

The per-person user setting `tasks.autoJoin` controls this behavior through the existing user-settings
routes. Only the JSON boolean `false` disables automatic joining; a missing or cleared value, `true`,
or any other JSON value enables it. Disabling preserves existing memberships and manual joining.
Enabling again joins missing active tasks. The owner still joins a newly created task even when
their automatic joining is disabled. Setting changes use the same last-write-wins rules and
owner-only `user_settings.updated` events as other settings; a stale write cannot change participation.
Standalone installations retain their existing behavior: their one person joins each new task.

Explicitly leaving a task suppresses automatic joining of that task for that person, including
across daemon restarts and disabling and re-enabling automatic joining. An explicit join clears
that suppression. Archived tasks are not automatically joined, but restoring a task makes it
eligible again for members who have not explicitly left it. Leaving a task before joining it also
records the suppression without emitting a membership event. Absences before this behavior was
introduced are not recorded leaves and are eligible for automatic joining.

**Mentioning a teammate.** In team mode a task's agent reads the team through its
`list_teammates` tool (each member's user ID, name, `role`, and `follows`; guests left out) and
mentions someone with `notify_teammate`, giving one sentence on why the task concerns them. The
mention never adds them to the task. It goes to that member's own Prioritizing as a waking notice
(see Spaces, "Notices"), which decides from what they do and follow whether the task belongs in
their sidebar; when it does, its `join_task` tool joins its person to the task. Such a join ignores
`tasks.autoJoin`, clears an explicit leave like a manual join, and marks the membership `unread`
with Prioritizing's reason; joining a task the member is already in keeps its place and marks it
unread again. A member without Stem Cells has no Prioritizing, so the mention adds nothing for
them. Agents write a person as `<happy-user id="…"/>` (see "Reference tags").

**Unread.** A membership's `unread` says what its member has not looked at yet, today only that
their Prioritizing added the task for them after a teammate's agent mentioned them. It is the
member's own: it travels on their membership in task responses, `GET /v0/tasks`, the desktop
bootstrap's `taskMemberships`, and their `task.joined` and `task.membership.updated` events, on
every connection that member uses, a paired phone included. `POST /v0/tasks/:taskId/read` clears
it, and so does `POST /v0/agents/:agentId/read` on the task's agent.

**Archival permission.** In standalone mode the installation's one person may archive and
unarchive every task. In team mode the task's owner and the team owner may; any other member
receives `403` with `code: "forbidden"`. A task without an owner can be archived only by the team
owner. Each task object tells the caller whether they may, through `canArchive`.

**Showing the owner.** Resolve `ownerUserId` through `GET /v0/users` for the owner's name and
photo placeholder, and fetch the photo itself from `GET /v0/users/:userId/photo`. In standalone
mode `ownerUserId` is `null`; the installation's one person is the profile, whose photo is
`GET /v0/profile/photo`.

**Mapping to workspaces and agents.** A task is one dedicated workspace plus one agent in it, all
with distinct IDs. The workspace is a real workspace, exactly like a bot's: it is fetched from
`GET /v0/workspaces/:workspaceId`, and its files, file tree, file revisions, terminals, Git state,
Git watch, proxy, and secret attachments all work by its workspace ID. Its `kind` is
`"task"`, its `taskId` names the task, its `botId`, `projectId`, and `parentId` are `null`, and its
`agents` array always contains exactly the task's agent. It is not a Git worktree; it is a plain
folder created empty, so its `base` is `null`. Its lifecycle belongs to the task — renaming,
archiving, reordering, and creating children through the workspace routes answer `409` — and it
appears in no project list, `GET /v0/workspaces` listing, or bootstrap `workspaces` array. While the
task is archived its folder routes answer `409`, as for an archived bot. The daemon emits no
`workspace.*` events for a task's workspace: its status follows the task, so a client holding the
workspace object refetches it after `task.updated`.

The agent is an ordinary agent and every agent endpoint works on it unchanged. Its `workspaceId`
names the task's workspace. It reports `userVisible: true`, `managedByAnotherAgent: false`, and
`canSendMessages: true` while the task is active, and its `orderKey` is `null`. Its lifecycle
belongs to the task: agent `archive`, `unarchive`, and `reorder` on a task's agent answer `409`.
It is the workspace's only primary conversation: `POST /v0/agents` refuses a task's workspace with
`409`. Shared-filesystem subtasks run in the task's workspace and appear in the task agent's
`subtasks` tree.

Tasks are a versioned resource. The task's `version` covers its own fields — name and archival.
Memberships are not versioned: a person's own list has one writer, and the latest reorder wins. The
embedded agent is independently versioned and changes through `agent.updated` without advancing
the task.

### The task object

```json
{
    "id": "t4k8m2q9w1e5r7y3u6i0o2p4",
    "name": "Fix login redirect",
    "folderName": "fix_login_redirect",
    "ownerUserId": "u1x2y3z4a5b6c7d8e9f0g1h2",
    "creatorAgentId": "a9b8c7d6e5f4g3h2i1j0k9l8",
    "workspaceId": "w5v4u3t2s1r0q9p8o7n6m5l4",
    "compute": { "type": "host", "path": "/Users/steve/Happy/Tasks/fix_login_redirect" },
    "status": "active",
    "canArchive": true,
    "agent": {
        /* the task's one agent, the full agent object */
    },
    "version": "01991f3a-5c1e-7000-8000-2f9a1b3c4d5e",
    "createdAt": 1755300000000,
    "updatedAt": 1755400000000,
    "archivedAt": null
}
```

Fields:

- `id` — stable task identifier.
- `name` — the human display name, 1–256 nonblank characters without ASCII control characters.
  The task's agent title follows it. A task created without a name is called `New Task` until it
  names itself; see `POST /v0/tasks`.
- `folderName` — the immutable snake_case folder name, unique across all tasks, archived ones
  included: lowercase ASCII letters, digits, and underscores, starting with a letter, 1–64
  characters.
- `ownerUserId` — the owner's Happy user ID, or `null`, as described above.
- `creatorAgentId` — the agent whose `create_task` call created the task, or `null`.
- `workspaceId` — the task's dedicated workspace, its own distinct ID.
- `compute` — where the task's folder lives, same shape as on projects, workspaces, and bots.
- `status` — `"active"` or `"archived"`.
- `canArchive` — whether the caller may archive and unarchive the task, under the archival
  permission above. It depends on the caller, so it appears in route responses and the bootstrap
  but not in `task.created` events, which reach every member alike, nor in `task.updated`
  `changes`. It never changes for a given caller and task, so a client keeps the value it read; a
  client that learns of a task only from `task.created` reads `GET /v0/tasks/:taskId` before
  offering archival. Older compatible daemons omit it.
- `agent` — the task's one agent, embedded in full. It merges by its own `version` through
  `agent.updated` events.
- `version` — the UUIDv7 concurrency version; see the basics.
- `createdAt`, `updatedAt`, `archivedAt` — lifecycle timestamps; `archivedAt` is `null` while
  active.

### The membership object

```json
{
    "taskId": "t4k8m2q9w1e5r7y3u6i0o2p4",
    "userId": "u1x2y3z4a5b6c7d8e9f0g1h2",
    "orderKey": "5",
    "joinedAt": 1755300000000,
    "unread": {
        "reason": "looped_in",
        "since": 1755400000000,
        "agentId": "f2g3h4j5k6l7m8n9p0q1r2s3",
        "note": "Refunds go through Stripe, which Steve follows."
    }
}
```

- `taskId` — the joined task.
- `userId` — the member's Happy user ID, or `null` for the standalone installation's one person.
- `orderKey` — an opaque sort key within this member's list; clients order the member's tasks by
  comparing these strings.
- `joinedAt` — when the member joined.
- `unread` — `null`, or what the member has not looked at yet: `reason` (`"looped_in"` today; the
  set may grow, and clients treat an unknown reason as plain unread), `since` (when it was marked),
  and optionally `agentId` (the agent that marked it, the member's Prioritizing) and `note` (its
  one-sentence reason, 1–500 characters, shown to the member). Additive: older daemons omit it, and
  clients treat a missing value as `null`.

Every task route answers only with the caller's own memberships. In team mode one member never
sees another member's memberships.

### `GET /v0/tasks`

Lists tasks with the caller's memberships.

Query `scope` is optional: `all`, the default, lists every task, archived ones included, oldest
`createdAt` first with the ID as the tie-breaker. `joined` lists only the tasks the caller joined,
in the caller's own order. Any other value is `400 invalid_request`.

Response — `200`: `{ "tasks": [ /* task objects */ ], "memberships": [ /* membership objects */ ] }`.
`memberships` is always every membership of the caller, in the caller's order, whichever scope was
requested, so a global list also shows which tasks the caller joined.

### `POST /v0/tasks`

Creates a task for the authenticated person: the row, its dedicated workspace with its folder,
and its one agent, together. The creator becomes the owner in team mode and joins the task at the
top of their list.

Request:

```json
{
    "id": "t4k8m2q9w1e5r7y3u6i0o2p4",
    "workspaceId": "w5v4u3t2s1r0q9p8o7n6m5l4",
    "agentId": "a9b8c7d6e5f4g3h2i1j0k9l8",
    "name": "Fix login redirect",
    "folderName": "fix_login_redirect",
    "mutationId": "..."
}
```

- `id` — optional client-supplied task ID; the retry key.
- `workspaceId`, `agentId` — optional IDs for the new dedicated workspace and its one agent.
- `name` — optional. A supplied name is deliberate and is never automatically replaced. Omitted,
  the task is created immediately usable as `New Task` and names itself, as described below. A
  supplied blank or invalid name is `400`.
- `folderName` — optional. Omitted, the daemon derives one from the name (lowercased,
  non-alphanumeric runs collapsed to underscores), starting from `task` without a name, and
  resolves a collision by appending a numeric suffix. A malformed folder name is `400`; one
  already taken by any task, archived included, is `409 conflict`.

Malformed IDs return `400 invalid_request`; omitted IDs are generated independently. For a new
task, all three must be distinct and unused by any task, bot, workspace or project root, or agent,
archived and hidden included (`409 conflict`).

Repeating `id` returns the current task unchanged (`201`), including after rename, archival, or
restart; other valid creation fields are ignored and nothing is joined again. Omitted child IDs
reuse the stored ones; mismatches return `409 conflict` with `currentVersion` and `task`. Retries
have no side effects or events. Identity checks and creation are atomic, and a folder-setup
failure rolls back creation without exposing entities or events.

Response — `201`: `{ "task": { ... }, "membership": { ... } }`, the caller's new membership.
Creation is complete when it answers: the folder exists, the workspace and agent exist, the agent
is `"idle"`, and the task is ready for its first message through `POST /v0/agents/:agentId/send`.
New creation emits `task.created`, `task.joined`, and `agent.created` once after commit, echoing
`mutationId`.

**Automatic naming.** For a task created without a name, by this route or by `create_task`, the
first accepted text-bearing message starts one durable asynchronous naming job: a person's message,
or the message from the agent that created it, which is how a `create_task` call's opening `text`
arrives. Naming uses that message alone and the same cheap, bounded inference as workspace naming,
and asks for a short title of the work, two to six words. Ordinary task naming continues
independently of the Stem Cells setting. Work created through enabled
Stem Cells instead uses a one-to-three-word subject name from the conversation's own model,
bounded to 32 characters. Message acceptance and the task's own
turn never wait for naming. Failure or timeout does not fail the message. A new naming job makes
at most three bounded attempts, waiting one second before its second attempt and five seconds
before its third. Pending jobs and their attempt budget survive daemon restarts; later messages
do not start another job. If every attempt fails, a bounded plain-text excerpt of that first
message supplies the name, within the same word and character limits. Text with no letters or
numbers supplies no fallback. Previously attempted naming is not backfilled or restarted.
The generated name updates the task and its agent title
together, publishing `task.updated` and `agent.updated`. The task's IDs, folder, and workspace
never change. An explicit rename always wins, including one committed while naming is running.

### `PATCH /v0/tasks/:taskId`

Renames the task. Requires `If-Match` with the task's `version`. Any authenticated person may
rename a task, as with bots; renaming is not restricted like archival.

Request: `{ "name": "Fix the redirect loop", "mutationId": "..." }`

Renaming also updates the task's agent title and permanently ends automatic naming for the task,
even when the submitted name equals the current one. The `folderName` is immutable: a request
carrying `folderName` is `400 invalid_request`.

Response — `200`: `{ "task": { ... }, "membership": { ... } }` with the new name, and a
`task.updated` event after commit, echoing `mutationId`. `404` when no such task exists, and the
usual `409` when `If-Match` names an older version.

### `GET /v0/tasks/:taskId`

Response — `200`: `{ "task": { ... }, "membership": { ... } }`. `membership` is the caller's
membership in the task, or `null` when the caller has not joined it. `404` when no such task
exists.

### `POST /v0/tasks/:taskId/join`

Joins the task, placing it at the top of the caller's list. The body is `{}` or
`{ "mutationId": "..." }`. Idempotent: joining a task the caller already joined changes nothing,
keeps its place, and emits no event.

Response — `200`: `{ "task": { ... }, "membership": { ... } }`. A new membership emits
`task.joined` after commit, echoing `mutationId`. `404` when no such task exists.

### `POST /v0/tasks/:taskId/read`

Marks the task read for the caller: clears their membership's `unread`. The body is `{}` or
`{ "mutationId": "..." }`. Idempotent: a task with nothing unread for the caller, or one they have
not joined, changes nothing and emits no event.

Response — `200`: `{ "task": { ... }, "membership": { ... } }`, the membership being the caller's
or `null`. A cleared mark emits `task.membership.updated` after commit, echoing `mutationId`.
`404` when no such task exists. Additive: older compatible daemons return `404`.

### `POST /v0/tasks/:taskId/leave`

Takes the task out of the caller's list. The body is `{}` or `{ "mutationId": "..." }`.
Idempotent: leaving a task the caller has not joined changes nothing and emits no event. Leaving
does not change the task, its owner, or anyone else's membership, and it never archives the task,
even when the owner or the last member leaves.

Response — `200`: `{ "task": { ... }, "membership": null }`. A removed membership emits
`task.left` after commit, echoing `mutationId`. `404` when no such task exists.

### `POST /v0/tasks/:taskId/reorder`

Moves the task within the caller's own list.

Request: `{ "afterId": "c3d4e5f6...", "mutationId": "..." }` — the joined task to place this one
after, or `null` to move it first.

Response — `200`: `{ "task": { ... }, "membership": { ... } }`. The moved membership receives a
fractional `orderKey` between its destination neighbours in the caller's list; every other
membership, the caller's neighbours and other members' lists included, is unchanged. Moving a task
to the place it already holds changes nothing and emits no event; otherwise the move emits
`task.reordered` after commit, echoing `mutationId`.

`404` when no such task exists. `409 conflict` when the caller has not joined the task, when
`afterId` names the task itself, or when `afterId` names a task that is not in the caller's list.

### `POST /v0/tasks/:taskId/archive`

Archives the task. Requires `If-Match` with the task's `version`; the body is `{}` or
`{ "mutationId": "..." }`. Idempotent — an already archived task answers the same way and stops
nothing.

Archiving has the same effect as the creating agent's `archive_task` tool and the same durability
contract as bot archival: the task agent's run is aborted and its background processes are
stopped, the agent is archived and its `canSendMessages` becomes `false`, and its history remains
readable. The folder is kept on disk — archival is logical, never a deletion. Memberships are
unchanged. Only an admin bot's [permanent deletion](#permanent-deletion) removes the folder and
the task.

Response — `200`: `{ "task": { ... }, "membership": { ... } }` with `status` `"archived"`; the
membership is the caller's, or `null`. Archival emits `task.updated` after commit, echoing
`mutationId`. `403 forbidden` when the caller may not archive the task, `404` when no such task
exists, and the usual `409` when `If-Match` names an older version.

### `POST /v0/tasks/:taskId/unarchive`

Brings an archived task back: its agent accepts messages again, with its history, folder, owner,
and memberships intact. Requires `If-Match`; the body is `{}` or `{ "mutationId": "..." }`.
Idempotent. The same callers may unarchive as may archive.

Response — `200`: `{ "task": { ... }, "membership": { ... } }` with `archivedAt` `null`, and a
`task.updated` event after commit, echoing `mutationId`. `403`, `404`, and `409` as for archive.

## Spaces

Spaces are the sidebar: one flat, ordered list of each person's work. A space is one row in it —
almost always a task, otherwise a bot (Happy among them) or a Surface — with an icon, a place, and
a visibility. Rows sit in flat
sections, and each section may show only its first few rows behind a "+k more" tail.

Each person has their own sidebar. In standalone mode it belongs to the installation's one person;
in team mode every route answers with the authenticated member's sidebar only, and `space.*`
events reach only that member's connections. Rows and sections are created by the daemon: the
first read makes a row for Happy, first in the list, and one row for every other active bot, joined
task, and joined Surface supported by Spaces that the person already has. Todo, document, and
calendar Surfaces wait until they contain content, unless attached under a row; other supported
kinds, including chats and references, are seeded on membership alone. New work gets a row when it appears; archived or left work loses it.

**Prioritizing.** Organisation belongs to Prioritizing, an ordinary subtask of Happy that runs
on the person's default model. It is enabled only when that person's opaque user setting
`happy-desktop.experiments.stemCells` is the JSON boolean `true`; missing, cleared, false, or
other values disable it. This setting is per authenticated member in team mode and belongs to
the installation's one person in standalone mode. Enabling it does not itself create work or
Prioritizing, and neither does reading the sidebar. While enabled, Prioritizing is created with the
person's first new work (`POST /v0/spaces/stem-cells`), when the person asks for a tidy
(`POST /v0/spaces/tidy`), or when Happy's onboarding starts it (see "Focus in onboarding"), the same
way Happy creates any subtask, and appears in Happy's `subtasks` like any other; until then there is
none, and nothing organises the sidebar. The person knows it as Focus: a new one is titled Focus,
and an existing one keeps its title. This chapter still calls it Prioritizing, and the API
identifiers that name it (`"prioritizing"` as an author, for one) stay. It has a
conversation of its own, shown as its own row (see "Prioritizing's row"), where the person may
write to it; it never writes into Happy's conversation. After a new conversation's first
message it gives the row an icon and decides where it belongs, and it tidies the list when asked.
Prioritizing runs when an event wakes it (see "Notices"), never on a timer and never because of its
own writes. Its placement, visibility, and section changes are not applied one by one: they wait
and apply together as one **batch** when its turn ends. Each batch adds one short line to
Prioritizing's conversation, such as "Moved Preview viewer release to Releases.", that names every
row it folded out of view, and is recorded with its diff. Only small changes apply this way; a
larger reorganisation is a proposal the person accepts or declines (see "Proposals"). Icons apply at once, because they move
nothing. A person's own changes through `PATCH` apply immediately and are never queued. Other
agents may ask Prioritizing to move a row through their `request_space_placement` tool; only
Prioritizing changes organisation. Disabling Stem Cells stops that person's Prioritizing,
discards its unapplied organisation changes and notices, and prevents further automatic
briefing, tool mutations, or batch application while disabled. Existing conversations and
names are left unchanged. Task, bot, and ordinary chat naming remain independent of this
setting and continue while it is disabled.

Where work goes and what stays in view are Prioritizing's judgement, made from its instructions.
The daemon enforces only what keeps the document whole and what the person did themselves (see
"Authorship").

**Order.** A client renders the shown rows with no section in `orderKey` order, then each section
in `orderKey` order with its shown rows in `orderKey` order, then the folded rows — the Older part
of the list — most recently updated first. Hidden rows are not shown. Order keys are opaque
strings compared within one section; moving a row changes only that row's key. Happy is an
ordinary row whose target is Happy's bot; it starts first and the daemon places new work after it.
The person may drag it like any other row.

**Task trees.** A task row is the root of the sidebar's existing task tree, built from data the
client already holds rather than from the space: the task's subtasks are its agent's `subtasks`
(see Agents), shown while their `status` is `"working"` or their `pendingQuestionId` is set; its
workspaces are the active workspaces whose `creatorAgentId` or `subtaskAgentId` is the task's
agent or one of those subtasks, shown while they exist. A bot row, Happy's included, is the root
of its bot's subtasks the same way; Prioritizing is one of Happy's. Their changes travel as
`agent.updated` and `workspace.*` events, not as `space.updated`.

### The space object

```json
{
    "id": "s3k8m2q9w1e5r7y3u6i0o2p4",
    "title": "Android 2.5 release",
    "icon": { "type": "library", "slug": "app-store-release" },
    "target": {
        "kind": "task",
        "id": "t4k8m2q9w1e5r7y3u6i0o2p4",
        "agentId": "a9b8c7d6e5f4g3h2i1j0k9l8",
        "workspaceId": "w5v4u3t2s1r0q9p8o7n6m5l4"
    },
    "sectionId": "c2n4b6v8m0q1w3e5r7t9y1u3",
    "orderKey": "5",
    "placement": "settled",
    "visibility": "shown",
    "working": false,
    "version": 4,
    "createdAt": 1755300000000,
    "updatedAt": 1755400000000
}
```

- `title` — the content's current name, or `null` while new work has not named itself yet;
  clients label such a row themselves, for example "New".
- `icon` — `null` until one is chosen, `{ "type": "stem_cell" }` for new work Prioritizing has not
  given an icon yet, `{ "type": "library", "slug": "..." }` for an icon from the desktop's icon
  library, or `{ "type": "image", "url": "/v0/bots/.../avatar" }` for the content's own picture,
  a daemon-relative path read with the same bearer token.
- `target` — what selecting the row opens. `kind` is `"task"` for nearly every row: new work and
  everything the person does is a task. `"bot"` names a bot's conversation — Happy's row and any
  other bot the person has — and `"surface"` a joined Surface. `"agent"` names a conversation that
  is neither a task's nor a bot's: Prioritizing's own row or Voice's own row, whose `id` and
  `agentId` are that agent and whose `workspaceId` is Happy's workspace. `id` is that task, bot,
  Surface, or agent. `agentId` is the conversation to show and `workspaceId` its
  workspace; both are `null` for a Surface. A client skips a row whose `kind` it does not know.
- `sectionId` — the section it is in, or `null`.
- `orderKey` — its opaque position within its section.
- `placement` — `"pending"` while Prioritizing has not placed new work yet, otherwise `"settled"`.
- `visibility` — `"shown"`, `"folded"` (in the Older part of the list), or `"hidden"`.
- `working` — whether the target's conversation is running now. Shortly after that conversation
  starts or stops running, aborted runs included, the daemon publishes the row again in
  `space.updated` with a greater `version` and `working` as it is then; runs that start and stop
  close together are published once.
- `version` — an integer that grows with every change to the row. Space events carry whole
  objects, so a client keeps the copy with the greater `version`.

### The section object

```json
{
    "id": "c2n4b6v8m0q1w3e5r7t9y1u3",
    "name": "Releases",
    "visibleCount": 3,
    "collapsed": false,
    "tailExpanded": false,
    "orderKey": "5",
    "version": 2,
    "createdAt": 1755300000000,
    "updatedAt": 1755400000000
}
```

- `name` — unique within the person's sidebar, ignoring case.
- `visibleCount` — show only this many rows and put the rest behind "+k more", or `null` to show
  all of them. Prioritizing chooses it.
- `collapsed`, `tailExpanded` — the person's own disclosure: the whole section closed, and the
  "+k more" tail opened.
- `orderKey`, `version`, `createdAt`, `updatedAt` — as on spaces.

A section with no rows stays until Prioritizing removes it.

### The batch object

```json
{
    "id": "b7x2c4v6b8n0m1q3w5e7r9t1",
    "note": "Tucked away Preview viewer release.",
    "noteMessageId": "m1q3w5e7r9t1y3u5i7o9p1a3",
    "changes": [
        {
            "spaceId": "s3k8m2q9w1e5r7y3u6i0o2p4",
            "title": "Preview viewer release",
            "from": {
                "sectionId": "c2n4b6v8m0q1w3e5r7t9y1u3",
                "sectionName": "Releases",
                "index": 0,
                "visibility": "shown"
            },
            "to": { "sectionId": null, "sectionName": null, "index": 0, "visibility": "folded" }
        }
    ],
    "appliedAt": 1755400000000
}
```

- `note` — the one line added to Prioritizing's conversation, written from the changes themselves.
- `noteMessageId` — that line's message `id` in Prioritizing's conversation (`target.agentId` of
  Prioritizing's row), or `null` when that conversation is unavailable. Daemons before
  `capabilities.sidebarProposals` added the line to Happy's conversation instead.
- `changes` — every row the batch moved, with its `title` at the time. `from` and `to` give its
  section, its zero-based `index` among the rows it was listed with (its section's shown rows,
  the unsectioned shown rows, the folded rows, or the hidden rows), and its `visibility`. A batch
  that only added or removed sections has no changes.

### `GET /v0/spaces`

The caller's whole sidebar, every visibility included.

Response — `200`:

```json
{
    "spaces": [
        /* space objects */
    ],
    "sections": [
        /* section objects, in order */
    ],
    "cursor": "01991f3a-..."
}
```

`cursor` is the event cursor this snapshot is current at; follow `space.*` events from it.

### Sidebar artifact

The experimental sidebar is one immutable-versioned `sidebar` artifact per person, owned by
Prioritizing and listed under Happy. The document is the sidebar's state: what it contains, in its
order, is exactly what the sidebar shows. The document is created lazily on the first
sidebar-document read; Prioritizing is created on that read or new work only while that person's
Stem Cells setting is enabled. Legacy sections, order keys, placement and
visibility keep their existing contract independently; document edits do not synchronize them.

**Grammar.** The entry is a single UTF-8 `index.html`, at most 128 KiB, 1,000 elements and a
depth of 16. It is one `<nav>` element holding a flat, ordered list of items. Every item may be
moved by the person anywhere in that list:

- `<happy-search/>` and `<happy-create/>` — the search and new-work controls. Each appears exactly
  once, at any position.
- `<happy-section id="…" label="…" collapsed="true"/>` — a titled heading over the items after it,
  up to the next `happy-section` or `happy-separator`. The heading and those items form the
  section's block, which moves as one unit. `label` is required, 1–256 characters, and not only
  whitespace. `collapsed="true"` folds those items. A section has no children.
- `<happy-separator id="…" pin="top|bottom"/>` — a plain visual rule with no title. It cannot fold
  and accepts no attribute other than `id` and `pin`; it ends the run of the section above it.
  `pin` is optional and absent when unpinned: `pin="top"` keeps everything above the separator in
  place, `pin="bottom"` everything below it. Any other value is rejected. The pin is the person's.
- `<happy-space id="…" expanded="true" fold-ribbon="hover|always">` — a row. A `happy-space` that
  is a direct child of `nav` names one of the person's spaces by space ID. A `happy-space` may
  contain only `happy-space` elements. A nested one names either a session by agent ID, whose
  parent must be the conversation of the enclosing node (the row's `target.agentId`, or the
  enclosing session), or an attachment: another of the person's spaces by space ID, such as a
  todo list, document, calendar, or chat Surface, or another task or bot row (see Attachments
  below). `expanded="true"` shows what is nested; otherwise it is folded.
- `<happy-widget id="…">…</happy-widget>` — a free-form card, the escape hatch for something the
  other items cannot say, such as a compact status card. Its content is arbitrary HTML: everything
  between the opening tag and the first `</happy-widget>`, kept exactly as written, at most 16 KiB
  of UTF-8 that may not contain `</happy-widget` in any letter case. An empty widget may be
  self-closing. The skeleton never interprets the content. A widget accepts no attribute other
  than `id` and has no switches; nothing nests in it, and it never nests in anything. Like a row,
  it may be moved or removed, it is folded away inside a collapsed section, and while shown it
  counts toward the visible budget. Clients render it isolated, as they render an artifact page:
  no token, no API, and no access to the app.
- Section, separator, and widget IDs are required, unique in the document, and 1–128 characters
  without whitespace or control characters. All three are direct children of `nav` only.
- `nav` and `happy-space` accept optional `fold-ribbon`: `"hover"` (default) shows a foldable row's
  fold ribbon only while pointed at; `"always"` shows it at the far right with the row's change
  count to its left. A row's own value overrides the `nav` value. Rows reserve no empty trailing
  slot; the change count sits flush right.

Boolean switches are written `"true"` or `"false"`; an absent switch is false. Attribute values are
always quoted; empty elements may be self-closing. Outside widget content, text, comments, other
elements, other attributes, duplicate IDs, unknown space IDs, and nested IDs that are neither
sessions of their parent nor the person's spaces are rejected, so HTML and XML parsers read the same skeleton. Spaces and sessions
absent from the document are not shown. Clients draw every element other than a widget's content
with trusted components; worktrees are drawn under the node whose agent works in them, and hidden
while that node is folded.

**Node IDs.** Every node has a stable ID, which is how every write below names it. Sections,
separators, rows, sessions, and widgets carry theirs in `id`; Search, Create, and the root are
named by their tags, `happy-search`, `happy-create`, and `nav`. Those three names are reserved and
are not valid `id` values. IDs are unique across the whole document.

**Canonical form.** The daemon stores the document in one canonical form: one element a line, two
spaces a level, attributes in the order shown above, false switches and default values left out,
empty elements self-closed, and widget content written verbatim between its tags. Serializing a
parsed canonical document gives back the same text, and any valid document parses to the same tree
as its canonical form. `@slopus/happy-agent-client` exports the parser, the serializer, the
operation applier, and the document differ the daemon itself uses (`parseSpaceSidebar`,
`formatSpaceSidebar`, `applySpaceSidebarOperations`, `diffSpaceSidebar`), so a client and the
daemon read, write, and apply the document identically.

Validation runs on every artifact write, including ordinary artifact tools. Rejection preserves the
last valid version and returns diagnostics `[{message, offset}]` (zero-based character offset).

**Attachments.** A space stands in exactly one place, because IDs are unique: at the top level, or
attached under one row or session. Attaching a space moves it there (`move` with
`{ "parent": "ID" }` or after a node nested there), or inserts it there when the document does not
hold it; a space never appears twice. An attachment carries its own sessions nested under it, as a
top-level row does. A session is never attached: it stands only under the conversation that is its
parent, so moving or inserting it under any other row or session is refused like any other
document rule.

- Taking an attachment out with `remove` — the person's or Prioritizing's, including a
  whole-document write that leaves it out — keeps it in the sidebar: it moves to the top level
  immediately after the top-level row that held it, and the operations applied and announced show
  that `move` instead of the `remove`. A client that takes an attachment out sends that move
  itself, so its optimistic document matches.
- When the attached space itself leaves (archived, or the person leaves its Surface), it is removed
  wherever it stands, as today.
- When the row or session holding attachments is archived or left, its attachments first move to
  the top level immediately after it, in their order, and it is then removed with its sessions.
- A todo, document, or calendar Surface row that was attached stays in the sidebar while it holds
  no content, until the person leaves that Surface. A chat Surface has a row while the person is a
  member, like a reference.

Revealing a row's sessions is an ordinary batch: a client inserts each of the row's sessions that
the document does not hold under the row (`insert` of `{ "type": "space", "id": AGENT_ID }` with
`{ "parent": ROW_ID }` for the first and `{ "after": PREVIOUS_ID }` for the rest) and sets
`expanded: true` on the row. A row's sessions are its agent's `subtasks` and collaborators, as
under "Task trees". The person's reveal records them as the sessions' place and the row's fold, so
Prioritizing never takes those sessions out of the document, folds the row, or folds a section over
them.

**First document.** The first document mirrors the person's classic sidebar: Search, Create,
Happy's row, the joined Surfaces in member order, `<happy-separator id="divider"/>`, then the
default sections `<happy-section id="bots" label="Bots"/>` with every other active bot in catalog
order and `<happy-section id="tasks" label="Tasks"/>` with the person's active tasks in membership
order. Each bot and task row, and Happy's, nests its active subtasks recursively in sibling order
and is `expanded="true"` when it has any. The default sections are present even without rows.
Until the person's first write or Prioritizing's next notice, sidebar responses include
`seed: true` and Prioritizing's writes are refused; a client may use one write to apply state only
it knows, such as the person's folds. A document stored in the earlier grammar (nested
`happy-section` with children, `ref`, `tree`, plain HTML) is rewritten once, as its own version, on
the next read: sections become flat `happy-section` headings keeping their IDs, labels, and
collapsed state, the former Older section (`id="older"`) becomes `<happy-separator id="older"/>`,
order is preserved, and plain HTML is dropped.

#### `GET /v0/spaces/sidebar`

Returns `200 {artifactId, version, html, spaces, agentIcons, seed?}`, where `version` is the
immutable artifact version number, `html` the document at that version, `spaces` the person's
ordinary space objects, and `agentIcons` maps session agent IDs to
`{ "type": "library", "slug": "..." }` icons Prioritizing chose (sessions get icons whether or not
they are shown). Daemons that predate `agentIcons` and `seed` omit them; clients treat a missing
`agentIcons` as empty and a missing `seed` as false.

Query `widgets=true` includes `happy-widget` elements in `html`. Without it they are left out of
`html`, so clients that predate widgets never meet one; `version` is the same either way. Any other
value of `widgets` is `400 invalid_request`.

#### `POST /v0/spaces/sidebar/operations`

The person's change to the document, as a batch of small operations keyed by node ID. Clients that
draw the sidebar from the document make every change through this route: a drag, a fold, a rename,
showing or hiding a session.

Request:

```json
{
    "mutationId": "c1x2v3b4n5m6q7w8e9r0t1y2",
    "baseVersion": 12,
    "operations": [
        { "type": "move", "id": "s3k8m2q9w1e5r7y3u6i0o2p4", "to": { "after": "releases" } },
        { "type": "set", "id": "releases", "collapsed": true },
        {
            "type": "insert",
            "node": { "type": "separator", "id": "quiet" },
            "to": { "after": "s9w1e5r7y3u6i0o2p4k8m2q3" }
        },
        { "type": "remove", "id": "a9b8c7d6e5f4g3h2i1j0k9l8" }
    ]
}
```

- `mutationId` — required; the client-chosen ID of this batch and its retry key.
- `baseVersion` — required; the document version the client made the operations against.
- `operations` — 1–1,000 operations, applied in order as one atomic batch.

A position `to` is exactly one of:

- `{ "after": "ID" }` — immediately after that node and everything nested in it, among its
  siblings. After a section heading is the first place in its block; to follow a whole block, name
  its last item.
- `{ "parent": "ID" }` — first among that row's or session's nested sessions.
- `{ "parent": null }` — first in the list.

The operations:

- `{ "type": "insert", "node": …, "to": … }` adds one node. `node` is
  `{ "type": "space", "id", "expanded"?, "foldRibbon"? }`,
  `{ "type": "section", "id", "label", "collapsed"? }`, `{ "type": "separator", "id", "pin"? }`, or
  `{ "type": "widget", "id", "html" }`. A row's nested sessions are inserted one by one after it.
  Search and Create cannot be inserted.
- `{ "type": "move", "id", "to" }` moves a node with everything nested in it. Moving a section
  moves its block: the heading and every item after it up to the next section or separator. A
  node cannot move into itself or into the block it carries. `nav` cannot move.
- `{ "type": "remove", "id" }` takes a node out with everything nested in it. Removing a section
  removes only its heading; the items of its block join the block above. Search, Create, and `nav`
  cannot be removed.
- `{ "type": "set", "id", … }` changes a node's switches and text: `collapsed` and `label` on a
  section, `pin` on a separator, `expanded` and `foldRibbon` on a row or session, `foldRibbon` on
  `nav`, and `html` on a widget. `foldRibbon: null` returns to the default; `pin: null` unpins. At
  least one field; a field the node's kind does not have is invalid.

Every operation must keep the document within the grammar as it applies: where each kind may
stand, unique IDs, depth, element count, and size. The batch's result must also satisfy the
document rules above, such as known space IDs and sessions nested under their parents.

**Rebase.** The batch applies to the current version, not to `baseVersion`, so a batch made
against an older version still lands wherever its operations make sense:

- A target or anchor that still exists is used where it is now, wherever other changes moved it.
- When an `after` anchor is gone, the node goes after the nearest node that preceded the anchor
  among its siblings in `baseVersion` and still sits under the same parent, and first under that
  parent when none does.
- Removing a node that is already gone changes nothing.

Only real conflicts are refused: moving or changing a node that is gone, inserting an ID that
exists now, a `parent` that is gone, or an `after` anchor whose parent is gone. Each is
`409 conflict` with `currentVersion` and `operationIndex`, the zero-based index of the first
operation that cannot apply, and the message names that operation. A batch that cannot apply even
to `baseVersion` is `400 invalid_request` with `operationIndex` and `diagnostics`; a result the
document rules refuse is `400 invalid_request` with `diagnostics`. A `baseVersion` newer than the
current version is `409 conflict` with `currentVersion`. A refused batch writes nothing.

Response — `200`:

```json
{
    "artifactId": "k2n4b6v8m0q1w3e5r7t9y1u3",
    "version": 13,
    "html": "<nav>\n  <happy-search/>\n  …\n</nav>\n",
    "operations": [
        /* the operations as applied */
    ]
}
```

- `version` — the document's version after the batch; unchanged when the batch changed nothing.
- `html` — the document at that version, widgets included.
- `operations` — what was applied: an anchor that fell back is replaced by the position used, and
  operations that changed nothing are left out.

A retry with the same `mutationId` and the same request returns the first answer and applies
nothing again, also when the retries race; the same `mutationId` with a different request is
`409 conflict`. The daemon recognizes at least each person's last 500 batches. The first batch
ends `seed`.

#### `PUT /v0/spaces/sidebar`

The whole-document write that clients predating operations use. It takes
`{html, baseVersion, mutationId}`, all three required, and returns the shape of
`GET /v0/spaces/sidebar` without widgets. A stale base is `409 conflict` with `currentVersion`;
there is no rebase. The daemon turns the difference between the current document and `html` into
operations by node ID with `diffSpaceSidebar` and applies them as the person's batch, so authorship
and events are those of `POST /v0/spaces/sidebar/operations`. Widgets are neither shown nor changed:
the request cannot contain one, and widgets it leaves out stay where they are. A retry with the
same mutation ID returns its committed version without writing again; reusing an ID for different
content is `409`. Invalid content is `400 invalid_request` with `diagnostics`. These details are
additional fields on the ordinary error envelope.

#### Authorship

For every node the daemon records who last set each of its aspects: its place (`insert`, `move`,
or `remove`), its fold (`collapsed` or `expanded`), its `label`, its `foldRibbon`, and a widget's
`html`. Inserting a node also sets what it starts with: a section's label and fold, a row's fold
and any `foldRibbon` it names, a widget's `html`. The author is the person (through these routes
and ordinary artifact updates) or Prioritizing. Lifecycle changes the daemon makes itself — inserting new work, removing archived
work — record no author, and a node they remove takes its authorship with it. Moving a section
records its heading only: the items in its block are carried and keep their own authorship. A
person's `set` records them as the author of every aspect it names, even when the value was
already the same and the operation changes nothing, so an explicit `expanded: true` on an open row
keeps Prioritizing from folding it.

Prioritizing never overrides the person: an operation of Prioritizing's that would change an aspect
the person last set, or would hide a session whose place the person last set, is dropped, as is one
that breaks Prioritizing's other rules below, and the rest of its batch still applies. Each dropped operation is reported to Prioritizing in a note. The
person's operations are never dropped. A separator's `pin` is only ever the person's: an operation
of Prioritizing's that sets, changes, or clears a pin, or inserts a pinned separator, is dropped, in
its batches and in its proposals alike, so accepting a proposal never changes a pin.

#### Changes and events

Every new version is announced after commit with `space.sidebar.changed`:
`{artifactId, previousVersion, version, author, operations, mutationId?}`. Applying `operations` to
`previousVersion` with `applySpaceSidebarOperations` gives `version` exactly. `author` is
`"person"`, `"prioritizing"`, or `"daemon"` for lifecycle changes; `mutationId` echoes the person's
batch, or the mutation of the request that caused a lifecycle change. A version that only changed
the artifact's title or background carries no operations. A client holding `previousVersion` applies
the operations; any other client reads the document again. The one-time rewrite of a document in the
earlier grammar happens before any client can read it and is not announced this way.

Every sidebar version also still emits the ordinary `artifact.updated` event for its artifact ID,
echoing the mutation ID when supplied, for clients that predate operations. A client that follows
`space.sidebar.changed` may ignore `artifact.updated` for the sidebar artifact.

For optimistic updates a client applies its unconfirmed batches with `applySpaceSidebarOperations`
on top of the last version it confirmed, sends them, and forgets each one when the
`space.sidebar.changed` event or response carrying its `mutationId` arrives.

Daemons that serve this route, widgets, `widgets=true`, and `space.sidebar.changed` report
`capabilities.sidebarOperations: true` from health. Older compatible daemons omit it, answer the
route with `404`, and reject `widgets` as an unknown query; clients then write whole documents
with `PUT`.

#### Prioritizing's writes

Ordinary artifact updates/tools accept optional `baseVersion`; it is required when the target is a
sidebar. A person's ordinary artifact update of the sidebar is a whole-document write like `PUT`.
Prioritizing may read and update its artifact using ordinary artifact tools. Its convenience
`edit_sidebar {baseVersion, edits:[{oldText,newText}]}` applies sequential exact-text replacements
(each old text must occur exactly once), making one version per call. Prioritizing reads and writes
HTML: the daemon compares its proposed document with the document it read, including its own queued
proposals, by node ID with `diffSpaceSidebar`, and queues the resulting operations. A stale
`edit_sidebar` base still applies when each old text occurs exactly once in the current document; a
stale whole-document update is a conflict. Queued operations wait for the end of Prioritizing's
turn, then apply through the same rebase and authorship rules, one version per proposal; a person's change in
the meantime no longer discards them. `get_sidebar_styles` returns the elements, their attributes
and children, and the rules above. `list_sidebar` returns the document, facts about each row, every
session under the person's rows with its parent, whether or not it is shown, and
`visibleRows: {shown, budget}`. `shown` counts the top-level rows and widgets outside collapsed
sections plus the sessions drawn under expanded rows, excluding Happy, new work before its first
message, and Prioritizing's own row; `budget` is 10, a target Prioritizing aims to stay within
rather than a limit the daemon enforces. A top-level row above the first separator whose place the
person last set is marked `keepInPlace: true`: what the person keeps at the top. The mark is
information for Prioritizing's instructions only; no edit or proposal is refused because of it.
`read_conversations {ids, turns?, replies?}` reads up to 12 conversations at once:
each ID is a space ID or a session's agent ID. For each it returns the row's title and when it was
last active, then its latest `turns` turns (default 4, at most 20), oldest first. A turn is one
message from the person or from another agent (such as a subtask's task or report) and the agent's
last `replies` text replies that answered it (default 1, at most 3); tool calls, their output,
reasoning, and hidden messages are left out. Each message is shortened to 1,200 characters, each
conversation to about 4,000, and the whole answer to about 24,000; conversations past that are
left out, and the answer ends with one line naming each of them by its heading, to be read again.
`set_space_icon` takes a
space ID or a session's agent ID.
A new Prioritizing is made on the person's default model from Settings at that model's default
effort. After that it runs on its own mode, as `GET /v0/agents/:agentId/mode` reports it: each notice
carries that mode while the installation still offers it, so a model or effort the person chooses
in its conversation stays. It
gives every session an icon but leaves sessions out of the document unless the person is likely to
want one shown, such as a session changing code in a repository or one waiting for their review.
It may propose a widget, rarely, where a compact card says more than a row. It may attach a
Surface under the work it belongs to when the person's messages clearly tie them together, by
nesting that Surface's row.

#### Prioritizing's row

Prioritizing's conversation has a row of its own, so the person can open it, read its notes and
proposals, and answer it. The daemon makes that space when it makes Prioritizing, and, on the first
start of a daemon that serves proposals, for every person whose Prioritizing predates its row. The
space has `target.kind: "agent"`, the icon `{ "type": "library", "slug": "goals" }`, the title
of Prioritizing's conversation, and `placement: "settled"`. Like any space, it shows only where the
document names it, and the daemon never inserts it into the document itself. The row is how the
person learns that a proposal exists, so Prioritizing keeps it in view without being asked. Its
instructions keep the row right below Happy unless the person put it elsewhere: on every run it
checks its row, reading the document again when it is unsure its view is current, inserts it with
an ordinary edit when it is missing, and moves it back when something came between. Inserting and
moving its own row apply without a proposal, even while `seed` is set, and apply when
Prioritizing's turn ends like its other edits. They are authored `prioritizing`, so the person may
move or remove the row like any other; once the person has placed it, Prioritizing's moves of it
are dropped, and once they have removed it, Prioritizing never puts it back. Where the row stands
is Prioritizing's decision: the daemon never inserts, moves, or repairs it in the document.

Prioritizing is told what to check, once: a new Prioritizing in its opening task; an upgraded one,
whose row the daemon has just made, in a notice that the daemon was updated. On the first start of
a daemon whose Prioritizing instructions are newer than the ones a Prioritizing last ran under, it
is told once that Happy Agent was updated and its instructions may have changed, and to check the
sidebar against them, starting with where its row stands. From its own conversation it then
decides what is still needed: whether to insert or move its row, and whether the person has already
seen, accepted, or declined a proposal. The row is not work: it never counts toward `visibleRows`,
and it is never briefed as new work. When Prioritizing is archived, its row leaves the
sidebar; the next Prioritizing gets a row of its own.

A message the person writes in Prioritizing's conversation is a request in their own words: the
changes Prioritizing queues while answering it apply as soon as that turn finishes, without the
limits below.

#### Focus in onboarding

Happy's onboarding (see "Onboarding in Happy's conversation") introduces Prioritizing as Focus,
Happy's sub-agent, and hands it the person's work. It requires the person's Stem Cells setting;
without it, Happy asks the person what they work on and starts nothing.

When the person presses `onboarding.discover`, the same transaction records their consent to
discovery and starts Prioritizing if they have none. `onboarding.workTell` starts it the same way,
without consent to discovery. A Prioritizing that onboarding starts is shown at once: its
conversation is inserted into the document as a session under Happy's row, and Happy's row gets
`expanded="true"` unless the person last set its fold, in one version authored `daemon`, committed
with the press. Its session icon is `goals`. From then on, where its conversation stands is
Prioritizing's decision as above.

Happy hands work to it with an ordinary `send_agent_message`, which Happy's conversation shows like
any message to another agent. Prioritizing answers as Happy's sub-agent: when a turn that received
a message from Happy ends, the daemon sends Happy one message from Prioritizing, as a collaborator's
report arrives, naming the proposal that turn made or revised, if any, and Prioritizing's last
reply. Happy's next turn starts from it; nothing waits in between. Prioritizing's other turns are
never reported to Happy.

While the person has consented to discovery, Prioritizing also has `discover_work`. It reads,
without writing anything, exactly the places Happy disclosed before the press: the projects in the
connected assistant's own session history (`~/.codex/sessions` for Codex, `~/.claude/projects` for
Claude Code), the first request of their recent sessions, and the last 30 days of commit subjects
and branch names in those projects. What it reads goes only to Prioritizing's model.

#### Voice's row

Voice (see Live voice sessions) is another subtask of Happy with a conversation of its own. The
daemon makes its space when it makes Voice: `target.kind: "agent"`, the icon
`{ "type": "library", "slug": "ai-agent" }`, the title of Voice's conversation, and
`placement: "settled"`. With a sidebar document, the daemon inserts it once, authored `daemon`,
right below Happy, or below Prioritizing's row when that follows Happy; the person may move or
remove it like any other row, and once they have removed it the daemon never puts it back. The row
is not work: it never counts toward `visibleRows`, Prioritizing never moves it, and it is never
briefed as new work. When Voice is archived, its row leaves the sidebar; the next Voice gets a row
of its own. Voice reads the sidebar but never writes it; its rearrangement requests reach
Prioritizing as placement requests or as Tidy.

#### What applies without a proposal

A batch applies only these operations of Prioritizing's:

- the first placement of new work: a `move` of a row that is still `placement: "pending"` and has
  never been placed, after any heading or row at the top level;
- inserting `<happy-section id="distractions" label="Distractions" collapsed="true"/>`, the folded
  section for work outside the person's current focus, when the document has none;
- inserting or moving its own row;
- changes nested under a row: inserting, moving, or removing a session or an attachment there, and
  `set` of `expanded` on a row or session.

Every other operation is dropped from the batch with a note that names it and says to propose
it: moving or removing any other top-level item; inserting any other section, separator, row, or
widget; `set` of `collapsed`, `label`, `foldRibbon`, or `html`; and any operation after which a row
that was already placed sits under a different heading. The rest of the batch still applies. When
the person asked for the change in their own words — through another agent's
`request_space_placement` with `personAsked`, or in Prioritizing's own conversation — this list does
not apply, and only the rules above and under "Authorship" do.

#### Proposals

A larger change is a proposal: a `sidebar_proposal` artifact that holds the person's sidebar with
the change made, shown to the person in Prioritizing's conversation. The person accepts it by
pressing Good to go, and asks for any change in their own words. Accepting applies it to the
sidebar as the person's own change. A proposal has
the sidebar's grammar, document rules, and limits, and its versions are immutable like any
artifact's. Version 1 is a copy of the sidebar as Prioritizing read it, its queued edits drawn in:
the proposal's base. Every later version is the proposed sidebar. Its `display` is
`"inline_expanded"`, and its source is Prioritizing's conversation. A person has at most one open
proposal.

Prioritizing writes proposals with `propose_sidebar {baseVersion, edits:[{oldText, newText}]}`,
exact-text replacements as in `edit_sidebar`. With no open proposal, the edits apply to the sidebar
as `list_sidebar` shows it, `baseVersion` is that version, and the call creates a proposal with its
base as version 1 and the edited document as version 2. With an open proposal, the edits apply to
its latest version, `baseVersion` is that proposal version, and the call makes the next version. A
stale `baseVersion` is a conflict, and so is an edit whose old text does not occur exactly once. An
edit that would change an aspect of a node that the person last set in this proposal is dropped
with a note, and the rest of the call applies. Everything else a proposal changes is Prioritizing's
decision, made from its instructions: it may regroup, fold, rename, or remove any row or section,
including those whose place the person last set in the sidebar, because accepting it is the
person's own change. The call presents the artifact (an `artifact`
presentation with `shows: "latest"`), so the proposal appears inline in Prioritizing's
conversation at its full height and again after every revision. The call returns the proposal's
artifact ID and version, not its reference tag, so that Prioritizing does not repeat the card in its
reply. `list_sidebar` also returns the open proposal, its document, the nodes the person last set in it, and the
decisions on the person's last proposals.

**New rows.** A proposal may also add work the person does not have yet: a new chat, with its name,
icon, and first message. `propose_sidebar` takes `newRows: [{ id, title, icon, why?, firstMessage }]`
beside its edits, where `id` is a name Prioritizing chose, `new-` followed by lowercase letters,
digits, and hyphens, and its edits place `<happy-space id="new-…"/>` where the row goes, at the top
level. The daemon replaces each such name with a space ID it reserves for the row, in the stored
document and in the proposal's `newRows`. That space does not exist until the proposal is accepted,
so `GET /v0/spaces` does not list it; a client draws a proposal row whose ID is not a known space
from `newRows`. A later revision may change a new row's title, icon, `why`, or first message by
naming its reserved ID, or add more. The person moves or removes a new row with the proposal
operations like any other; nothing else of it is theirs to change, and the first message is never
read from the document. Accepting creates exactly the new rows still in the latest version, each a
task chat with that space ID, named and iconed as proposed from the start rather than as new work
that names itself, placed where the proposal puts it, and sent its first message as the person's
message on the person's default model, read-only. A row whose chat cannot be created is skipped.

**Archive offers.** A proposal may also offer to archive work that looks finished. In a proposal
document, and only there, `<happy-section id="archive" label="Archive"/>` heads the rows offered
for archiving: the rows of its block, up to the next section or separator. Only top-level task rows
may stand in that block; any other row there is refused like any other document rule. Which tasks
to offer is Prioritizing's judgement, and nothing in the block is archived unless the person accepts. The person
keeps a row by dragging it
out of the block in the proposal, or offers one by dragging it in. Accepting archives each task in
the block as `POST /v0/tasks/:taskId/archive` archives it, as the person and with the person's
archive authority; a task the person may not archive, or one that is already archived, is
skipped and stays in the sidebar. The block's heading is never applied to the sidebar, and archived
rows leave it as archived work always does.

**Shipped work.** Prioritizing may archive one task by itself, at once, with
`archive_shipped_task {spaceId, shipped}`, only once its work has explicitly shipped or finished:
it is on the main branch or released, or the person said it is done, and nothing in it still
waits. `shipped` names what shipped in one sentence. Whether work shipped is Prioritizing's
judgement; the daemon checks only that the row is one of the person's own top-level task rows and
that the person may archive the task, and archives it as `POST /v0/tasks/:taskId/archive` does,
with the person's archive authority. Its row leaves as archived work always does, and the person
can unarchive it. Everything else that looks finished is offered through a proposal.

A client shows a `sidebar_proposal` inline as a sidebar drawn from its document with the same
components as the sidebar, never as raw HTML, and draws no controls in it: it is a preview. While it
is open, the client follows its latest version; once it is decided, the client shows its final
version and its status. Ordinary artifact updates of a proposal are refused with
`400 invalid_request`; only `propose_sidebar` and the routes below change one.

The decision is a chat action in the message that shows the proposal, Prioritizing's reply in its
own conversation or Happy's message in onboarding:

```html
<menu>
    <button data-action="sidebar.proposalAccept" data-proposal="ARTIFACT_ID" data-emphasis>
        Good to go
    </button>
</menu>
```

Pressing it is an action message (see `send`) with `params: { "proposal": "ARTIFACT_ID" }`. In the
press's own transaction it accepts the proposal exactly as the accept route below does, as the
person and with the press's message `id` as its `mutationId`, and the conversation's model answers
it with the outcome. `result` is `{ "proposal": "ARTIFACT_ID", "started": [{ "spaceId", "agentId" }] }`.
Only the newest offer of the action is live, as for every action, so a Good to go shown with an
earlier revision is a conflict. A proposal that is not open, or not the person's, is `409 conflict`
without `message`. It is pressable in Happy's conversation and in Prioritizing's own, and only in
standalone mode. There is no decline control: the person types what to change, and Prioritizing
revises the proposal or leaves it.

Happy shows a proposal in its own message with
`<figure data-sidebar-proposal="ARTIFACT_ID"></figure>`: the client draws that proposal's latest
version live from `GET /v0/spaces/sidebar/proposals/:artifactId` and
`space.sidebar.proposal.changed`, as the same preview. Any other client draws it as any unknown
figure.

The proposal object:

```json
{
    "artifactId": "p4k8m2q9w1e5r7y3u6i0o2p4",
    "version": 3,
    "baseVersion": 12,
    "status": "open",
    "createdAt": 1755300000000,
    "decidedAt": null,
    "acceptedVersion": null,
    "newRows": [
        {
            "id": "n7d2k9q4w1e5r7y3u6i0o2p4",
            "title": "Release CI",
            "icon": { "type": "library", "slug": "flaky" },
            "why": "Three failing runs this week",
            "firstMessage": "Look at why Release CI keeps failing and draft a read-only plan. Change nothing until I approve."
        }
    ],
    "started": []
}
```

- `artifactId`, `version` — the proposal's artifact and its latest version.
- `baseVersion` — the sidebar version the proposal was drafted from.
- `status` — `"open"`, `"accepted"`, or `"declined"`.
- `decidedAt` — when the person accepted or declined it, or `null` while it is open.
- `acceptedVersion` — the sidebar version that accepting it made, or `null`.
- `newRows` — optional additive: the new rows the proposal has offered, each with its reserved
  space `id`, `title`, `icon`, `why` (a few words on where the work was seen, or `null`), and
  `firstMessage`. A row that the latest version no longer holds is not created. Older daemons omit
  it; treat a missing list as empty.
- `started` — optional additive: once accepted, the chats accepting created, as
  `[{ "spaceId", "agentId" }]`. Older daemons omit it.

`GET /v0/spaces/sidebar` also returns `proposal`, the person's open proposal object, when one
exists. Daemons without proposals omit it, and so does a daemon when no proposal is open.

##### `GET /v0/spaces/sidebar/proposals/:artifactId`

Returns `200 {proposal, html}`, where `html` is the proposal's latest version with widgets
included. A proposal that does not exist or belongs to another person is `404`.

##### `POST /v0/spaces/sidebar/proposals/:artifactId/operations`

The person's edit of an open proposal. It takes the request of `POST /v0/spaces/sidebar/operations`,
with `baseVersion` naming a proposal version, and answers in the same shape, with the proposal's
artifact ID and version. Rebase, conflicts, document rules, and retries by `mutationId` are those of
the sidebar route. The new version is authored by the person, and the daemon records which nodes'
aspects the person set in this proposal; Prioritizing's later revisions keep them. A proposal that
is no longer open is `409 conflict`. The person's edit wakes Prioritizing (see "Notices").

##### `POST /v0/spaces/sidebar/proposals/:artifactId/accept`

Request: `{ "mutationId": "..." }`, required. Accepting applies the difference between the
proposal's version 1 and its latest version to the current sidebar, as the person's batch: the
operations come from `diffSpaceSidebar` with widgets included, and they rebase from version 1 as
`POST /v0/spaces/sidebar/operations` rebases from its `baseVersion`. An operation that no longer
applies — its node is gone, its ID now exists, or its result breaks a document rule — is skipped,
and the rest apply. The version is authored by the person, records them as the author of
everything it set, publishes `space.sidebar.changed` with author `"person"` and the `mutationId`,
and ends `seed`. When nothing changed in the meantime, the sidebar becomes exactly the proposal's
latest version.

Response — `200 { "proposal": { ... }, "sidebar": { "artifactId", "version", "html", "operations" }, "archived": [ ... ], "started": [ ... ] }`,
where `sidebar` takes the shape of the `POST /v0/spaces/sidebar/operations` response,
`archived` lists the space IDs whose tasks accepting archived, and `started`, optional and
additive, lists the new rows' chats as `{ "spaceId", "agentId" }`. Accepting creates the new rows
before it applies the operations, so each is inserted where the proposal puts it. The proposal
becomes `"accepted"`. A retry with the same `mutationId` returns the first answer and applies
nothing again. A proposal that was already decided under another mutation is `409 conflict`.
Prioritizing is told at once.

##### `POST /v0/spaces/sidebar/proposals/:artifactId/decline`

Request: `{}` or `{ "mutationId": "..." }`. The proposal becomes `"declined"`, and the sidebar is
not changed. Response — `200 { "proposal": { ... } }`. Declining a declined proposal answers the
same again; an accepted one is `409 conflict`. Prioritizing is told at once, and it does not
propose the same change again unless the person asks.

##### Proposal events

Every new proposal version and every change of a proposal's status is announced after commit with
`space.sidebar.proposal.changed`: `{proposal, previousVersion, author, operations, mutationId?}`.
For a new version, applying `operations` to `previousVersion` with `applySpaceSidebarOperations`
gives `proposal.version`, and `author` is `"person"` or `"prioritizing"`. A new proposal's first
event has `previousVersion` 1. For a status change, `operations` is empty, `previousVersion` equals
`proposal.version`, and `author` is `"person"`. The ordinary `artifact.created` and
`artifact.updated` events are still emitted. Like other `space.*` events, these reach only the
proposal's person.

#### Attaching from a conversation

`attach_to_my_row` lets a conversation put something under its own row, for example when the person
asks it to "make me a todo list for this". It is offered only to a conversation that has a row in
the sidebar of a person whose Stem Cells setting is enabled, or to a session under such a row;
every other conversation, including every conversation of a person without Stem Cells, is offered
nothing new. Its arguments are exactly one of:

- `spaceId` — one of that person's rows, such as another task or an existing Surface's row;
- `surfaceId` — an existing Surface of type `todo`, `document`, `calendar`, or `chat`; its row is
  made when the person has none;
- `create: { kind, name }` — a new Surface, `kind` one of `"todo"`, `"document"`, `"calendar"`, or
  `"chat"`, made as `POST /v0/surfaces` makes it with the person as creator (`null` in standalone
  mode), so the person joins it. In team mode the other members join it by their own
  `surfaces.autoJoin` preference; people outside the installation cannot be invited, so a chat with
  someone else is a chat Surface they are a member of.

It attaches the space under the calling conversation's row (a session's root row), first among
what is nested there, and sets `expanded: true` on that row unless the person last set its fold.
The change applies at once as one version authored `prioritizing`, under the same authorship
rules: it is refused, changing nothing, when it would move a space whose place the person last set,
Happy, or new work before its first message, or when the calling row is not in the document. Once
the person moves the attachment, nothing moves it back. The result names the space and, for a
Surface, its ID and reference tag, so the agent can fill it with the ordinary Surface tools. It is
durable: a repeated call creates and attaches once. It requires Auto or Full access, because it
writes shared Surface data, and a call that creates a Surface is reviewed in Auto.

Fold state lives in the document; clients report nothing else about what the person sees.

#### Notices

Prioritizing learns what happened through notices, delivered as automatic messages in its own
conversation. These events **wake** it:

- the person's committed sidebar changes, through any route, including a `set` that changes no
  value;
- new work or a new session appearing under the person's rows, with its title when it has one;
- new work's first message, whoever writes it: the person, or the agent that created the task
  (shortened to 1,500 characters), and every later message the person writes in it while it still
  waits for its first placement, since that message may make its subject clear;
- the person's edit of a proposal, and their accepting or declining it;
- another agent's `request_space_placement`, "Tidy now", and the person's own messages to it;
- in team mode, a teammate's message in one of the person's rows or a session under one, named as
  that teammate's rather than the person's, with the agent's last text reply before it;
- in team mode, a teammate's agent mentioning the person through `notify_teammate`, with the task,
  the agent's reason, and the person's own `role` and `follows` (see Tasks, "Mentioning a
  teammate").

Other notices wait for the next wake without causing one: every later message the person themselves writes in
placed new work, or in a session under it, with the agent's last text reply before it (both shortened,
without tool calls, and only the latest three notices per row kept); a session under such work
that stops, with its last reply; work that finished or paused, left, or was renamed; and the
operations dropped from Prioritizing's own batches. Work that did not start as new work through
`POST /v0/spaces/stem-cells` has its first message told but no later messages forwarded.
Prioritizing's own writes — its batches, its proposals, its row — never wake it.

A wake waits two seconds for the rest of its burst, and every waiting notice goes into one message;
while Prioritizing is still deciding, the wake waits for its turn to end, so a burst causes at most
one more run. Wakes and notices are durable: a restart in between delivers them afterwards. A
notice stays owed until a run that received it ends without a failure; when the run fails, such as
for a model without credentials, its notices are delivered again with the next wake, which a
failure itself never causes.

New spaces are inserted immediately after Happy's top-level `happy-space` element, below everything
nested in it, or immediately after the section or separator named at creation. When Happy's row is
absent, nested, or inside a collapsed section, they are inserted immediately after
`<happy-create/>` instead. Archived or left spaces, and archived sessions, are removed together
with everything nested under them. Lifecycle changes publish `space.sidebar.changed` and ordinary
artifact events.
