- `version` — the UUIDv7 resource version, used with `If-Match`.

Who may read a story:

- an agent's story — only the person it was posted for (`authorUserId`), while it is not deleted,
  whether it is in its ring or ended;
- a draft — only its author;
- a posted story in team mode — every member with Stories on;
- a posted story in standalone mode — the installation's one person;
- a deleted story — its author only, as a tombstone.

Anyone else receives `404 not_found` for it and every route under it.

### Frames

Every frame is drawn on the same **canvas**: a 9:16 portrait rectangle, 1080 × 1920 in canvas
units, the size of a phone screen. Clients fit the canvas to the screen and letterbox what is
left in black, so a frame, its media, and its overlays look the same on every client. Positions
are fractions of the canvas, never of the screen or of the media.

- `id` — the frame's CUID2, stable for the life of the story.
- `kind` — `"image"`, `"video"`, or, on daemons with `capabilities.agentStories`, `"artifact"`: a
  live artifact filling the whole canvas (see "Artifact frames").
- `width`, `height`, `scale`, `thumbhash` — the media's size in pixels after EXIF orientation, its
  pixels per point, and its ThumbHash, measured the way chat media measures images. `scale` is
  absent when it is 1. Videos have `null` `width`, `height`, and `thumbhash` until the daemon
  measures videos; clients then take them from the player.
- `durationMs` — a video's length when known, otherwise `null`. Clients show an image for five
  seconds and a video for its length, at most 60 seconds. `width`, `height`, `thumbhash`, and
  `durationMs` are `null` on an artifact frame.
- `asset` — the media the frame shows, by identity:
    - `{ "type": "chat_media", "agentId", "toolCallId", "index" }` — one item of a completed tool
      call's `media` presentation, as served by
      `GET /v0/agents/:agentId/tool-calls/:toolCallId/media/:index`.
    - `{ "type": "artifact_file", "artifactId", "version", "path" }` — one image or video file of an
      exact, immutable artifact version, as served by
      `GET /v0/artifacts/:artifactId/versions/:number/files/*path`. Media uploaded from a device for
      this story is kept this way, as a file of the story's own artifact (see "Making a story by
      hand").
    - `{ "type": "artifact", "artifactId", "version" }` — an artifact itself, shown live: the frame
      of `kind` `"artifact"`. `version` is a version number to pin, or absent to always show the
      artifact's latest version. Any artifact type may be shown except `story` and `sidebar`.
- `available` — whether the daemon still holds the asset. It becomes `false` when the asset's
  artifact is deleted, or when the daemon no longer holds the copy of a conversation's media. A
  conversation being archived does not change it, because archiving keeps the conversation's
  history and media. A client shows an unavailable frame as its background reading "This photo is
  no longer available" or "This video is no longer available", with its overlays. A posted story
  none of whose frames is available leaves the feed.
- `placement` — where the media sits on the canvas, as a transform (see "Overlays"). At scale `1`
  with no rotation, the media is fitted whole inside the canvas, centred at `x` and `y`: a
  screenshot fills the width and leaves bands above and below. Larger scales crop it. When it is
  omitted on a write the daemon uses the fitted placement.
- `background` — two `#RRGGBB` colours drawn as a top-to-bottom gradient behind the media, filling
  the bands. When it is omitted on a write, the daemon picks them from the image's dominant colours
  at its top and bottom edges; for a video it uses `["#000000", "#000000"]`.
- `overlays` — at most 32, in drawing order, the last on top. See "Overlays".

A frame's bytes are served by `GET /v0/stories/:storyId/frames/:frameId`, never by the asset's own
route: the viewer of a story does not need to be able to read the conversation or artifact the
media came from. Posting a story is its author sharing exactly those frames with its audience.

#### Artifact frames

An artifact frame shows an artifact as a live, interactive page that fills the canvas, so a story
can hold a small app the viewer can try. Artifacts are global — everyone using the installation
may read every artifact — so a viewer reads it with the ordinary artifact routes, never through
`/frames/:frameId`, which answers `404` for it. A client draws it exactly as it draws an inline
`artifact` presentation of that artifact and version (the latest when `version` is absent),
including the artifact data bridge of an `html` page and the artifact proxy of a live-URL page.

The page lays out in a viewport of 360 × 640 CSS pixels, scaled uniformly to fill the canvas, so it
looks the same on every client. `placement` is ignored and `background` is drawn behind it, black
by default. Touching or pointing into the page interacts with it instead of moving between frames,
and pauses the frame's timer until the pointer leaves or the touch ends; only the left and right
tenth of the canvas still move between frames. An artifact frame shows for ten seconds of
untouched time. When the artifact is deleted, or a pinned version does not exist, the frame is
unavailable and shows its background reading "This page is no longer available".

### Overlays

Overlays are drawn live by the client over the frame from the data below; the daemon never draws
them into the media. Every overlay has an `id` (a CUID2 the client chooses when it adds one, stable
afterwards), a `type`, and a `transform`:

- `transform.x`, `transform.y` — the overlay's centre, as fractions of the canvas's width and
  height, from the top-left corner. Values from `-0.5` through `1.5` are kept, so an overlay may
  hang off an edge.
- `transform.scale` — its size, from `0.05` through `10`. For text, `1` sets a base font size of
  1/16 of the canvas's width; for links, the pill's height at `1` is 1/12 of the canvas's width;
  for stickers and emoji, it is the fraction of the canvas's width the sticker's longer side takes;
  for artifact windows and round videos, the fraction of the canvas's width their width takes.
- `transform.rotation` — clockwise rotation in radians, from `-π` through `π`.

The types:

- `text` — `text` is 1–2,048 characters of plain text, newlines kept. `style.font` is one of
  `"sanFrancisco"`, `"typewriter"`, `"avenirNext"`, `"courierNew"`, `"noteworthy"`, `"georgia"`,
  `"papyrus"`, or `"snellRoundhand"`; clients draw it with the matching system font, falling back
  to their own sans-serif when it is missing. `style.fill` is how the colour is used: `"regular"`
  draws the text in it; `"filled"` draws a rounded box in it behind black or white text, whichever
  contrasts; `"semi"` does the same with the box at half opacity; `"stroke"` outlines the text in
  it with white text inside; and `"blur"` draws white text over a frosted box. `style.color` is a
  `#RRGGBB` colour. `style.align` is `"left"`, `"center"`, or `"right"`. `style.width` is the
  fraction of the canvas's width the text wraps at before scaling, from `0.1` through `1`.
- `link` — `url` is an absolute `http` or `https` URL of at most 2,048 characters, without a user
  name or password; `label` is 1–64 characters, or `null` to show the URL's host. `style` is
  `"white"`, `"black"`, `"transparent"`, `"blur"`, or `"custom"` with a `#RRGGBB` `color`. Clients
  draw it as a pill with a link glyph; tapping it in the viewer shows the URL with an Open button,
  and the browser opens only after that.
- `sticker` — `packId` and `stickerId` name a sticker, as described under Stickers. Clients draw
  it with that sticker's file, animated and looping when its format is animated. A sticker the
  daemon no longer serves is left out of the drawing; the overlay itself is kept.
- `emoji` — `emoji` is one emoji, a single extended grapheme cluster, drawn with the client's own
  emoji font.
- `reaction` — a round speech bubble holding one emoji, `emoji`, which viewers tap to react with
  it (see "Reactions"). `dark: true` draws a dark bubble instead of the white one, and
  `flipped: true` puts its tail on the right instead of the left; both are optional and false when
  absent. `transform.scale` is the fraction of the canvas's width the bubble takes. Once the story
  is posted, the bubble shows under its emoji how many people reacted with that emoji, from
  `reactions`, when there are any. A frame holds at most five reaction overlays.
- `artifact` — a live window onto an artifact, on daemons with `capabilities.agentStories`:
  `artifactId`, an optional `version` to pin (absent always shows the latest), and `aspectRatio`,
  the window's width over its height, from `0.25` through `4` (`0.8` when omitted on a write). The
  page lays out 360 CSS pixels wide and `360 / aspectRatio` tall, scaled uniformly to the window,
  with corners rounded at 1/27 of the canvas's width. It is drawn and interacts like an artifact
  frame (see "Artifact frames"), within the window. Any artifact type may be shown except `story`
  and `sidebar`; a deleted artifact or a missing pinned version is left out of the drawing, and
  the overlay itself is kept. A frame holds at most three artifact overlays.
- `round_video` — a round video, Telegram style, recorded with the webcam or the camera, on daemons
  with `capabilities.agentStories`. `media` is the video, a file of the story's own artifact:
  `{ "type": "artifact_file", "artifactId": <story id>, "version", "path" }` as read, under
  `media/<overlayId>` with the file's extension. A write places a new recording with
  `{ "type": "upload", "uploadId", "name" }` from `POST /v0/artifact-uploads` instead, and keeps an
  existing one by sending `media` back as it was read. The upload must be an MP4, QuickTime, or
  WebM video of at most 64 MiB. `durationMs` is its length, 1–60,000, as the recording client
  measured it. `mirrored: true` flips it horizontally, for a front camera; optional, false when
  absent. Clients draw it as a circle whose diameter is `transform.scale` of the canvas's width,
  the video centre-cropped to a square, with a thin white rim. It plays from its start with sound
  when the frame shows, the frame stays up at least as long as it plays, and it holds its last
  picture afterwards. Its bytes are served by `GET /v0/stories/:storyId/overlays/:overlayId/media`.
  A frame holds at most one round video, and only a person adds one; agents cannot.

A client ignores an overlay of a type it does not know.

The viewer shows one progress segment per frame across the top of the screen, for the author's
stories in order. Tapping the right third advances and the left third goes back; holding pauses
and hides the overlays' chrome; swiping down closes.

### Making a story by hand

Media from a device is uploaded first, with `POST /v0/artifact-uploads`, one upload per file.
Creating or editing a story then places each upload as a frame, and the daemon keeps its bytes as
a file of the story's own artifact version, under `media/<frameId>` with the file's extension. From
then on the frame's asset is that file, `{ "type": "artifact_file", "artifactId": <story id>, ... }`,
like any other.

The person may also choose media from their conversations. `GET /v0/stories/media` lists what
they may choose from one conversation.

### `GET /v0/stories`

The caller's own stories, newest first. Query parameters: `status` (optional; `draft`, `posted`,
or `deleted`, comma-separated for several, default `draft,posted`), `cursor` (optional, an opaque
cursor from a previous page), and `limit` (optional, `1`–`100`, default `50`). Only stories whose
`author` is the caller are listed; agents' stories posted for them are not, and are read through
the feed's `rings` instead.

Response — `200`:

```json
{
    "stories": [
        /* story objects */
    ],
    "nextCursor": null,
    "cursor": "01991f3a-..."
}
```

`nextCursor` reads the next page, or is `null` on the last one. `cursor` is the event cursor this
read is current at. A client's editor uses this route to return to a draft.

### `POST /v0/stories`

Makes a draft by hand.

Request:

```json
{
    "id": "s7k2m9q4w1e5t8y3u6i0o2p5",
    "caption": "Friday demo",
    "frames": [
        {
            "id": "f1a2b3c4d5e6f7g8h9i0j1k2",
            "upload": { "uploadId": "u8p2l4o6a1d3e5f7g9h0j2k4", "name": "IMG_0042.jpg" },
            "overlays": []
        },
        {
            "asset": {
                "type": "chat_media",
                "agentId": "a9b8c7d6e5f4g3h2i1j0k9l8",
                "toolCallId": "k8j7h6g5f4d3s2a1q0w9e8r7",
                "index": 1
            },
            "overlays": []
        }
    ],
    "mutationId": "..."
}
```

- `id` — optional client-chosen CUID2. A repeated `id` returns the story as it now stands with
  `201` and changes nothing, so a retry after a lost response never makes a second story.
- `caption` — optional; see the story object.
- `frames` — required, one to ten. Each has an optional `id`, optional `placement`,
  `background`, and `overlays` (see "Frames"), and exactly one of:
    - `upload` — an upload from `POST /v0/artifact-uploads` and the file's `name`, whose extension
      decides what it is. The upload must decode as a PNG, JPEG, GIF, WebP, or AVIF image of at most
      32 MiB, or be an MP4, QuickTime, or WebM video of at most 256 MiB. Clients convert HEIC photos
      to JPEG before uploading. It is used once: the story that places it removes it in the same
      change.
    - `asset` — a `chat_media` or `artifact_file` asset the caller may read, which must be an image
      or a video, or an `artifact` asset naming an artifact that exists and is not a `story` or
      `sidebar` (see "Artifact frames").

`overlays` may hold `artifact` and `round_video` overlays on daemons with
`capabilities.agentStories`. A round video's `upload` is checked and used once like a frame's; a
kept round video must name a file of this story's own artifact that it already holds.

The caller becomes `authorUserId`, `createdBy` is the caller, `source` is `null`, and `status` is
`"draft"`. Response — `201`: `{ "story": { ... } }`, and `story.created` after commit, echoing
`mutationId`. An unknown, expired, or already used upload, an asset the caller cannot read, an
asset that is not an image or video, and media over its limit are `400 invalid_request`, and the
message names the frame. Nothing is made when any frame fails.

### `GET /v0/stories/media`

Media the caller may put in a story, from one conversation. Query parameters: `agentId`
(required), `cursor` (optional), and `limit` (optional, `1`–`100`, default `30`).

Response — `200`:

```json
{
    "items": [
        {
            "asset": {
                "type": "chat_media",
                "agentId": "a9b8c7d6e5f4g3h2i1j0k9l8",
                "toolCallId": "k8j7h6g5f4d3s2a1q0w9e8r7",
                "index": 0
            },
            "kind": "image",
            "name": "login-fixed.png",
            "width": 1440,
            "height": 900,
            "scale": 2,
            "thumbhash": "3PcNNYSFeXh/d3eld0iHZoZgVwh2",
            "createdAt": 1755300000000
        }
    ],
    "nextCursor": null
}
```

Items are the images and videos the conversation's completed tool calls presented, followed by
the image and video files of artifacts it presented, newest first, each once. Artifact items carry
an `artifact_file` asset pinned to the version that was presented. A client shows each item with
its existing media URL. `404` when the caller may not read the conversation.

### `GET /v0/stories/:storyId`

Response — `200`: `{ "story": { ... } }`.

### `PATCH /v0/stories/:storyId`

Edits a draft or a posted story. Requires `If-Match` with the story's current `version`. Only the
author may edit; anyone else receives `403 forbidden`. Nobody edits an agent's story, its person
included: they repost it instead (see `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/repost`), so a `PATCH` of one is
`403 forbidden`.

Request — any of:

```json
{
    "caption": "Login redirect loop: fixed.",
    "frames": [
        /* the whole new frame list, as for POST */
    ],
    "mutationId": "..."
}
```

`frames`, when present, replaces the whole list. A frame that keeps its `id` and gives neither
`upload` nor `asset` keeps the media it had, so reordering frames and editing overlays never sends
media again. A frame `id` that is new must carry `upload` or `asset`.

Editing never changes `status`: a draft stays a draft until it is posted, and a posted story keeps
its `postedAt` and `expiresAt`. A change makes a new artifact version and answers
`200 { "story": { ... } }`, with `story.updated` after commit. A request that changes nothing
makes no version and emits nothing. A stale `If-Match` is `409 conflict` with `currentVersion` and
the current `story`. Editing a deleted story is `409 conflict`.

### `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/post`

Posts a draft. Only the author may post; anyone else, including the agent that drafted it,
receives `403 forbidden`. No tool posts a story.

Request:

```json
{ "artifactVersion": 3, "mutationId": "..." }
```

`artifactVersion` is the version the person was looking at when they pressed Post. When it is not
the story's latest version, the post is `409 conflict` with the current `story`, so a person never
posts a version they have not seen; the client shows the latest version and the person presses
Post again.

Posting sets `status` to `"posted"`, `postedAt` to now, and `expiresAt` to 24 hours later, and
answers `200 { "story": { ... } }`, with `story.posted` after commit. Posting a story that is
already posted answers `200` with it unchanged and emits nothing. Posting a deleted story is
`409 conflict`. A draft with no available frame is `409 conflict`, and the message says the media
is no longer available.

### `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/delete`

Deletes a draft or a posted story. Requires `If-Match`. Only the author may delete. The body is
`{}` or `{ "mutationId": "..." }`.

The story becomes a `"deleted"` tombstone: it leaves the feed and every other reader, and its
frames are no longer served. The author can still read the tombstone. Response — `200`:
`{ "story": { ... } }`, with `story.deleted` after commit. Deleting a deleted story answers `200`
and emits nothing.

An agent's story is deleted by the person it was posted for, who is its author for this route; it
leaves the ring and the conversation's inline story shows "This story was deleted".

### `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/seen`

Records that the caller has seen this posted story and every earlier story by the same author.
The client calls it as soon as it shows a story's first frame, not at its end. The body is `{}`.

Seen state is one mark per viewer and author, the newest story of that author the viewer has
seen, by `postedAt`. A call naming a story older than the mark changes nothing. Response — `200`:
`{ "story": { ... } }` from the caller's view, with `story.seen` to the caller's own connections
after commit, so their other devices update the ring. An author's own stories are always seen.
Marking a draft is `409 conflict`.

For an agent's story the mark is per viewer and ring instead: the newest story in that ring
(`ringAgentId`) the person has seen, by `postedAt`, whichever agent of the task tree posted it.
`story.seen` then carries `ringAgentId` too. An agent's story starts unseen, its person not being
its author.

### Reactions

Each person who may read a posted story has at most one reaction to it: one emoji. They react by
tapping a reaction overlay, which reacts with its emoji, or with the heart in the viewer's bottom
bar, which reacts with ❤️; pressing and holding the heart offers other emoji. Tapping the same
emoji again removes the reaction, and reacting with another emoji replaces it. The author does not
react to their own story; the viewer's bottom bar shows them `viewCount` instead. The person an
agent's story was posted for may react to it like anyone else's; the agent sees the reaction
through `list_stories`.

Everyone who may read the story sees its `reactions` counts. Only the author sees who reacted,
through `GET /v0/stories/:storyId/viewers`. Reacting also marks the story seen.

#### `PUT /v0/stories/:storyId/reaction`

Sets the caller's reaction. Request: `{ "emoji": "❤️", "mutationId": "..." }`, where `emoji` is a
single extended grapheme cluster that is an emoji. Response — `200`: `{ "story": { ... } }` from
the caller's view, with `story.reacted` after commit. Setting the reaction the caller already has
answers `200` and emits nothing. Reacting to a draft or a deleted story, or to one's own story, is
`409 conflict`.

#### `DELETE /v0/stories/:storyId/reaction`

Removes the caller's reaction. The body is `{}` or `{ "mutationId": "..." }`. Response — `200`:
`{ "story": { ... } }`, with `story.reacted` after commit; removing a reaction the caller does not
have answers `200` and emits nothing.

#### `GET /v0/stories/:storyId/viewers`

The people who have seen the author's own posted story, with their reactions. Only the author may
read it; anyone else receives `404`. Query parameters: `cursor` (optional) and `limit`
(optional, `1`–`100`, default `50`).

Response — `200`:

```json
{
    "viewers": [
        {
            "userId": "u9z8y7x6w5v4u3t2s1r0q9p8",
            "name": "Steve Korshakov",
            "photo": "/v0/users/u9z8y7x6w5v4u3t2s1r0q9p8/photo",
            "thumbhash": null,
            "reaction": "❤️",
            "reactedAt": 1755400000000
        }
    ],
    "nextCursor": null
}
```

Viewers who reacted come first, most recent reaction first, then the others by name. `reaction`
and `reactedAt` are `null` for a viewer who has not reacted. `photo` and `thumbhash` follow the
feed's author fields.

### `GET /v0/stories/:storyId/frames/:frameId`

The bytes of one frame, served from the asset it names. The response follows
`GET /v0/agents/:agentId/tool-calls/:toolCallId/media/:index` exactly: the file itself with its
media type and size, a content-digest `ETag` honouring `If-None-Match`,
`Cache-Control: private, max-age=31536000, immutable` and `Vary: Authorization`,
`Content-Disposition: inline`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, the sandboxing
`Content-Security-Policy`, and single byte ranges for video.

`404` when the caller may not read the story, when the story is deleted, when it has no such
frame, or when the frame is not available. Because a frame's asset never changes, neither does the
response for one URL; replacing a frame's media gives it a new frame ID.

### The inline draft

`draft_story` presents the story it makes or revises with an `artifact` presentation and
`shows: "latest"`:

```json
{
    "type": "artifact",
    "artifactId": "s7k2m9q4w1e5t8y3u6i0o2p5",
    "version": 1,
    "artifactType": "story",
    "title": "Login redirect loop: fixed.",
    "shows": "latest"
}
```

A client shows a `story` artifact inline as a story drawn from the story object with the same
components as the full-screen viewer, on its 9:16 canvas, never as raw HTML and
never as a tool call. It reads the story with `GET /v0/stories/:storyId` and follows it live
through `story.*` events.

While the story is a draft and the viewer is its author, two plain text buttons sit beside it:

- **Post** — posts the version shown, with `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/post`.
- **Edit** — opens the story editor on the draft. Saving sends `PATCH` and keeps it a draft; the
  inline story follows the new version, and Post is still needed.

Anyone else who reads the conversation sees the draft without buttons, labelled "Draft". Once
posted, the inline story shows its final version labelled "Posted", and opening it opens the
viewer. Once deleted, it shows "This story was deleted".

### `GET /v0/stories/:storyId/overlays/:overlayId/media`

The bytes of one `round_video` overlay, served exactly as `GET /v0/stories/:storyId/frames/:frameId`
serves a frame, single byte ranges included. `404` when the caller may not read the story, when it
is deleted, or when it has no such round video.

### `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/repost`

Starts the caller's own story from an agent's story: an ordinary draft, which the person edits in
the story editor and posts to their audience like any story of theirs. Only the person the agent's
story was posted for may repost it, ended or not; anyone else receives `404`. Reposting a story
that is not an agent's is `409 conflict`.

Request — `{}`, or:

```json
{ "id": "s2w4e6r8t0y2u4i6o8p0a2s4", "mutationId": "..." }
```

`id` is optional and works as on `POST /v0/stories`: a repeated `id` returns the draft as it stands
and makes nothing new. The draft copies the agent's story's caption and frames — new frame and
overlay IDs, the same assets, placements, backgrounds, and overlays — so its frames name the same
media without copying bytes, and artifact frames and windows stay live. Its `author` and
`createdBy` are the caller, its `source` is `null` because the person made it, and `repostOf`
names the agent's story. Response — `201`: `{ "story": { ... } }`, with `story.created` after
commit echoing `mutationId`. The reposted story itself is unchanged and stays in its ring.

The repost is then edited with `PATCH` and posted with `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/post` as usual:
editing may add round videos and any other overlay, and keeps `repostOf`, which no write can set or
change.

### `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/feedback`

Feedback on an agent's story, typed in the viewer: an ordinary message from the caller to the agent
that posted it (`author.agentId`, not the ring's root), quoting the frame. Only the person the story
was posted for may send it; anyone else receives `404`. It is `409 conflict` for a story that is
not an agent's, and when the agent no longer accepts messages, such as when it is archived.

Request:

```json
{
    "id": "m6n8b0v2c4x6z8l0k2j4h6g8",
    "frameId": "f1a2b3c4d5e6f7g8h9i0j1k2",
    "text": "Make the archive button red",
    "mutationId": "..."
}
```

- `id` — optional client-chosen CUID2 of the message, as on `POST /v0/agents/:agentId/send`; a
  repeated `id` sends nothing new.
- `frameId` — optional; the frame the viewer was on. Absent quotes the story as a whole.
- `text` — required, 1–8,000 characters.

The daemon sends `text` to the agent exactly as `POST /v0/agents/:agentId/send` would, on the mode
the agent last ran with (`409 conflict` when it has never run), preceded by one quoting line the
same for every client:

```text
> Feedback on your story "Focus's ideas, right where they apply" (s7k2m9q4w1e5t8y3u6i0o2p5), frame 2 of 3
Make the archive button red
```

The quoted title is the story's caption cut to its first line and 80 characters, or "Untitled"; the
frame part is left out without `frameId`. Response — `202`:
`{ "agentId": "...", "messageId": "..." }`. The agent answers in its own conversation, where the
client may open it. Feedback does not mark the story seen or change it.

### "Post it": `draft_story`

`draft_story` is a fixed common tool, offered to every ordinary agent conversation whose person has
Stories on and to no other.

| Argument      | Meaning                                                                                                                     |
| ------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `frames`      | Optional, one to ten `{ "toolCallId", "index" }` or `{ "artifactId", "version", "path" }` from this conversation, in order. |
| `caption`     | Optional, at most 1,024 characters.                                                                                         |
| `overlays`    | Optional, per frame position: `text`, `link`, `sticker`, and `emoji` overlays, as above.                                    |
| `storyId`     | Optional. A draft this call already made in this conversation, to revise.                                                   |
| `baseVersion` | Required with `storyId`: the `artifactVersion` the revision is made against.                                                |

The tool's description tells the model that "post it" and similar requests mean making a draft for
the person to post, not posting; to choose the one or two images or videos that best show the
outcome the person cares about, preferring the final result over intermediate attempts; and to
write a short caption in the person's words, without inventing results.

Without `frames`, the daemon chooses: the latest one or two images or videos this conversation
presented, newest last, preferring images the agent presented with `present_files` or generated
over images it only read. A frame named in `frames` must have been presented in this conversation;
any other is refused, and so is a conversation with nothing to choose from. The author is the
conversation's person, and `createdBy` the agent.

A revision makes a new version of the draft when nobody has changed it since `baseVersion`. If the
person edited the draft after that version, the call fails and says so, and the agent reads the
story again before trying once more; the person's edits always win. A story that is posted or
deleted cannot be revised. A call is keyed by its tool call, so it makes nothing a second time
after a restart.

The call presents the story inline (see "The inline draft") and returns its story ID and
`artifactVersion`, not a reference tag, so the agent does not show it twice. It needs no review in
Auto, because a draft is visible only to its author and nothing is posted; it is refused in Read
only.

### Agent stories

An agent posts a story to say it shipped something: a release went out, a change was merged, an
artifact was published, there is something the person can try. The story is the same object as a
person's, made from the same frames and overlays, with `author` naming the agent. It goes straight
into the agent's ring, with no draft and no Post button, and is visible only to the person it was
posted for: the conversation's person, as for `draft_story`, or, for a subtask no person has
written in, the person of its nearest ancestor that has one. Teammates see it only if that person
reposts it.

**Rings.** A ring is drawn around a sidebar row's icon. It belongs to the row's agent — a task's or
a bot's own agent — and holds the live stories of that agent and of every subtask below it, so
subtasks' stories roll up into their task's or bot's ring (`ringAgentId`). A session row shows no
ring. Coloured segments are stories the person has not seen and grey ones are seen; a row with no
live story has no ring. Clicking the ring opens the viewer on the ring's first unseen story (or its
first story when all are seen); clicking the row's text opens the conversation as before. The
viewer plays the ring's stories oldest first, then closes. The ring never spins or animates
progress.

**Ending.** Agents' stories have no 24-hour clock. A story leaves its ring only when it ends:

- **superseded** — a later `post_story` names it in `supersedes`. Any live story in the same ring
  may be superseded, whichever agent of the tree posted it;
- **withdrawn** — `withdraw_story` names it, again within the same ring;
- **archived** — the ring's task or bot is archived. Archiving a subtask ends nothing: its stories
  stay in the root's ring until they are superseded, withdrawn, or the root is archived.

Ending is final. Whether an agent supersedes or withdraws, and when, is its own judgement, made
from its tools' instructions; the daemon only applies what it is told, within the ring.

**The viewer.** A client shows an agent's story in the same viewer as anyone's, with the agent's row
icon and conversation title in the header, a text field reading "Tell the agent what to change…"
that sends `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/feedback` for the frame shown, the heart, and a repost button
(↻) that calls `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/repost` and opens the editor on the draft it returns.

**Tools.** Every agent with no parent (a task's or a bot's own agent among them) and every subtask
is offered these fixed common tools while the person its stories are for, as above, has Stories on.
Hidden subagents and Prioritizing are not. `set_status` alone is narrower: only a task's or a bot's
own agent is offered it. Subtasks, other subagents, and project or workspace sessions are not.

| Tool             | Arguments                                                          | Does                                                                    |
| ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `post_story`     | `frames`, `caption`, `overlays` as for `draft_story`; `supersedes` | Posts an agent's story to this conversation's ring for its person       |
| `withdraw_story` | `storyIds`, 1–10 live stories in this conversation's ring          | Ends them as withdrawn                                                  |
| `list_stories`   | none                                                               | The ring's live stories: who posted each, caption, when, seen, reaction |
| `set_status`     | `text`, 1–120 characters on one line, or `null` to clear           | Sets the `statusLine` shown under this agent's row or session title     |

`post_story` takes the frames `draft_story` takes, and one more kind: `{ "artifactId", "version" }`
without `path`, an artifact this conversation presented, shown live as an artifact frame; without
`version` it always shows the latest. Its overlays add `{ "type": "artifact", "artifactId",
"version", "aspectRatio", "transform" }`, a live window onto such an artifact. Without `frames`, the
daemon chooses as for `draft_story`. `supersedes` lists live stories in the same ring that the new
one replaces; they end in the same change that posts it, and an ID that is not a live story of
that ring fails the call and posts nothing. The result names the story and lists the ring's
remaining live stories.

`post_story` and `withdraw_story` need no review in Auto: the story is visible only to the person,
and nothing reaches anyone else. `set_status` and `list_stories` need none either. Read only
refuses `post_story`, `withdraw_story`, and `set_status`. Each call is keyed by its tool call, so a
call repeated after a restart posts, ends, or sets nothing a second time.

`set_status` is always in the conversation's tool list rather than found through tool search, and
the conversation's instructions ask the agent to set it early and change it at key moments, only
where the tool is offered. How long to make it is guidance to the agent, never enforced: the call
accepts any `text` the wire allows, and nothing is truncated.

The tools' instructions decide when to post, and they are the product: post only when something
real shipped that the person can see or try, never progress; supersede the agent's earlier story
whenever it posts again, and withdraw stories that went stale — superseded or reverted work, or
something the person already acknowledged by watching, reacting, or replying; keep at most one live
story per agent and, across a task tree, only the freshest. Captions and status lines are written
for someone who is not reading the code: what changed for them, in plain words, without commit
hashes, versions, test counts, file names, or scores such as "14/14" unless the person asked for
them.

### `GET /v0/stories/feed`

Everything a client needs to draw the stories row: one circle per author, in rank order, with the
caller's own circle always first.

Response — `200`:

```json
{
    "authors": [
        {
            "userId": "u1x2y3z4a5b6c7d8e9f0g1h2",
            "name": "Kirill Dubovitskiy",
            "photo": "/v0/users/u1x2y3z4a5b6c7d8e9f0g1h2/photo",
            "thumbhash": "1QcSHQRnh493V4dIh4eXh1h4kJUI",
            "isSelf": true,
            "storyIds": ["s7k2m9q4w1e5t8y3u6i0o2p5"],
            "unseenCount": 0,
            "draftCount": 1,
            "latestPostedAt": 1755300000000
        },
        {
            "userId": "u9z8y7x6w5v4u3t2s1r0q9p8",
            "name": "Steve Korshakov",
            "photo": "/v0/users/u9z8y7x6w5v4u3t2s1r0q9p8/photo",
            "thumbhash": null,
            "isSelf": false,
            "storyIds": ["s1q2w3e4r5t6y7u8i9o0p1a2", "s3d4f5g6h7j8k9l0z1x2c3v4"],
            "unseenCount": 1,
            "draftCount": null,
            "latestPostedAt": 1755400000000
        }
    ],
    "rings": [
        {
            "agentId": "a9b8c7d6e5f4g3h2i1j0k9l8",
            "storyIds": ["s5g6h7j8k9l0z1x2c3v4b5n6"],
            "unseenCount": 1,
            "latestPostedAt": 1755400500000
        }
    ],
    "stories": {
        "s7k2m9q4w1e5t8y3u6i0o2p5": {
            /* story object */
        }
    },
    "cursor": "01991f3a-..."
}
```

- `authors` — in the order the row shows them. The first is always the caller, with
  `isSelf: true`, even when they have no story: that circle is "My Story", with a "+" badge at its
  bottom right. The others are the authors with at least one posted, unexpired story that has an
  available frame, in rank order (see "Ranking").
- `userId` — the author's Happy user ID, or `null` for the one person in standalone mode.
- `name` — the author's current name.
- `photo` — a daemon-relative path to the author's photo, read with the same bearer token
  (`/v0/profile/photo` for the standalone person), or `null` when they have none; `thumbhash` is
  its placeholder. Without a photo, clients draw the author's initials.
- `storyIds` — the author's posted, unexpired stories in viewing order, oldest first. Empty only
  for the caller's own circle when they have none.
- `unseenCount` — how many of `storyIds` the caller has not seen. Always `0` on the caller's own
  circle.
- `draftCount` — on the caller's own circle, how many drafts they have; `null` on every other.
- `latestPostedAt` — when the author's newest story in `storyIds` was posted, or `null`.
- `rings` — optional additive, always emitted by daemons with `capabilities.agentStories`: one
  entry per sidebar ring with at least one live story posted for the caller, newest
  `latestPostedAt` first. `agentId` is the ring's agent (`ringAgentId`): match it to the sidebar
  row whose target `agentId` it is. `storyIds` are its live stories in viewing order, oldest first;
  `unseenCount` how many of them the caller has not seen; `latestPostedAt` when the newest was
  posted. A ring absent from the list has nothing to show. An agent's stories never appear in
  `authors`.
- `stories` — every story `storyIds` names, in `authors` and `rings`, from the caller's view, keyed
  by ID.
- `cursor` — the event cursor this read is current at; follow `story.*` events from it.

A client keeps rings live from events: `story.posted` of a story with an agent `author` adds it to
the end of its `ringAgentId`'s ring, unseen; `story.ended` and `story.deleted` take it out; and
`story.seen` with a `ringAgentId` marks every story of that ring posted at or before the named one
as seen.

How the row behaves, the same in every client:

- Each circle is the author's avatar, with their first name beneath it; the caller's reads
  "My Story".
- A circle with `unseenCount > 0` has a coloured ring; one with none unseen has a thin grey ring.
  The caller's own circle has a ring only when they have a story.
- Tapping another author's circle opens the full-screen viewer on that author's first unseen story,
  or on their first story when all are seen. The viewer plays each story's frames in turn, then
  the author's next story, then the next author in the row, swiping sideways between authors
  with a cube turn. Its controls are described under "Overlays".
- On the caller's own circle, tapping "+" opens the editor on a new draft. Tapping the circle opens
  the editor too when they have no story, and otherwise the viewer on their own stories, which
  shows each story's `viewCount` at the bottom. A client that shows `draftCount` offers the drafts
  in the editor.
- When the viewer shows a story, the client calls `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/seen`.

### Ranking

Ranking is computed per viewer on every feed read, without inference, from data the daemon
already holds. Authors are ordered in three groups: first the caller; then authors with at least
one unseen story; then authors whose stories are all seen. Within a group, authors are ordered by
importance, then by `latestPostedAt`, newest first.

An author's importance to the viewer is the highest importance among the author's stories in the
feed. A story's importance adds up:

- **Closeness of its work** — the story's source conversation is one the viewer takes part in: the
  task's or Surface's conversation the viewer joined, or a row shown in the viewer's sidebar.
  Stories made by hand have no source and get nothing here.
- **Closeness of its author** — how much the viewer works with the author over the last 14 days:
  tasks both joined, Surfaces both are members of, and conversations both wrote in.
- **Interest** — of the author's stories posted in the last 14 days, the share the viewer has
  seen.
- **Freshness** — halves every six hours since `postedAt`.

The weights are the daemon's and may change; clients use the order they are given and never sort
again. In standalone mode the row is the caller's circle alone.

### Events

All `story.*` events are journaled, follow the version-chain rules, and reach only the
connections of people who may read the story as it is after the change.

- `story.created` — a new draft, in full. It reaches the author only. An agent's story is never
  a draft: `post_story` emits only `story.posted`, reaching the person it was posted for.
- `story.updated` — `storyId`, `previousVersion`, `version`, the whole `story`, and optional
  `mutationId`.
- `story.posted` — the whole `story`. It reaches every member of its audience, who reads the feed
  again or inserts the story into the author's circle.
- `story.deleted` — `storyId` and `version`. It reaches everyone who could read the story before
  it was deleted.
- `story.seen` — `authorUserId` and `storyId`, the viewer's new mark for that author. It reaches
  only the person who saw it. The author's `viewCount` changes without an event; the author's
  client reads the story again when it shows the count. For an agent's story it also carries
  `ringAgentId`, and the mark is for that ring.
- `story.ended` — an agent's story left its ring: `storyId`, `version`, `ringAgentId`, `endedAt`,
  `endReason`, and the whole `story`. It reaches the person the story was posted for. A story
  superseded by a new one ends in the same change that posts it, and its `story.ended` is
  delivered right after the new story's `story.posted`.
- `story.reacted` — `storyId`, the story's new `reactions`, and optional `mutationId`. It reaches
  everyone who may read the story; the reacting person's own copy also carries their new
  `myReaction`. The author's client reads `viewers` again when it shows them.

A story that expires emits nothing: clients drop it when its `expiresAt` passes, and the next feed
read leaves it out.

### Haptics

Clients play the same haptic feedback at the same moments, so stories feel the same everywhere. On
iOS they use the system feedback named below. On desktop they play the closest feedback where the
hardware supports it, such as a Force Touch trackpad on a Mac, and otherwise play nothing. The
daemon never plays haptics.

| Moment          | When                                                                     | iOS feedback         |
| --------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------- |
| `story.open`    | The viewer opens from a circle or an inline story.                       | Light impact         |
| `story.advance` | The viewer moves to another frame, story, or author.                     | Selection changed    |
| `story.post`    | `POST /v0/stories/:storyId/post` answers `200`, not when Post is tapped. | Success notification |
| `story.delete`  | A confirmed deletion answers `200`.                                      | Warning notification |
| `story.react`   | The viewer taps a reaction bubble or the heart, as the reaction appears. | Light impact         |
| `sticker.drop`  | A sticker or emoji lands on a frame from the picker.                     | Medium impact        |
| `overlay.snap`  | A dragged overlay snaps to a guide, once each time (see below).          | Rigid impact, light  |
| `overlay.trash` | A dragged overlay reaches the delete target.                             | Heavy impact         |

A refused post or deletion plays the error notification instead.

The editor's guides are the canvas's vertical and horizontal centre lines and, for stickers, lines
inset 1/20 of the canvas's width from each edge. A dragged overlay's centre snaps to a guide within
2% of the canvas's width, and its rotation snaps to multiples of 45° within about 2°; it stays held
until the drag moves past twice that distance, and a client shows the guide line while it holds.

## Stickers

The daemon serves a catalog of sticker packs for the story editor's picker, and each person's
recently used stickers. Story overlays name a sticker by its pack ID and sticker ID.

**Packs live outside the agent.** The daemon ships no packs and no sticker files: nothing from a
pack is in the package, the binary, or the installer. It serves whatever packs its sticker folder
holds, `<agent home>/stickers`, which configuration owns. Each pack is one folder named by its ID,
holding a `pack.json` manifest and the pack's files; an optional `order.json` at the top of the
folder lists pack IDs in the order the picker shows them, and packs it leaves out follow in title
order. The daemon reads the folder at startup and when it changes. A pack whose manifest does not
validate, or whose files do not match their size and digest, is left out. How packs get into the
folder is outside the API.

Stickers are part of Stories and follow its setting: for a person with Stories off, every route
under `/v0/stickers` answers `404 not_found`.

### The picker

Clients draw the picker the same way, as Telegram draws its sticker panel:

- A search field at the top. Typing searches with `q`; an emoji category chip under it searches
  with `emoji`. While a search is active, its results replace the scroll below as one grid.
- Below it, one continuous vertical scroll. It starts with a **Recently used** section, when the
  person has recents, with a ✕ that clears them. Then every pack follows in catalog order, each
  under a centred, uppercase header of its `title`, with its stickers in a grid beneath.
- The grid has `max(5, floor((width + spacing) / (70 + spacing)))` columns for packs of kind
  `"stickers"` and the recents, and `max(8, floor((width + spacing) / (40 + spacing)))` for packs
  of kind `"emoji"`, where `width` is the grid's width in points and `spacing` the gap between
  cells; cells are square and stickers are fitted inside them.
- A floating switch at the bottom selects **Stickers** or **Emoji**. Stickers shows packs of kind
  `"stickers"`; Emoji shows the system emoji by category, for `emoji` overlays, followed by packs
  of kind `"emoji"`.
- Tapping a sticker places it at the centre of the frame, plays `sticker.drop`, and records it
  with `POST /v0/stickers/recents`. Pressing and holding shows it large.

Animated stickers play in the grid while visible and stop when scrolled away. A `tgs` sticker is a
Lottie animation; clients play it with a Lottie renderer, reading `?as=lottie` when theirs cannot
read gzip. A `webm` sticker is a VP9 video with an alpha channel; clients that cannot decode VP9
with alpha through the platform, iOS among them, decode it in software.

### The pack object

```json
{
    "id": "utyaduck",
    "title": "Duck",
    "kind": "stickers",
    "thumbnail": "AgAD8gADVp29Cg",
    "stickerCount": 40,
    "version": "a34d668493927d74"
}
```

- `id` — the pack folder's name: 1–64 characters from `a–z`, `0–9`, `_`, and `-`.
- `title` — the pack's name, shown uppercase as its header.
- `kind` — `"stickers"`, shown large in the Stickers tab, or `"emoji"`, custom emoji shown at text
  size in the Emoji tab.
- `thumbnail` — the ID of the sticker that stands for the pack.
- `stickerCount` — how many stickers it holds.
- `version` — an opaque string that changes whenever the pack's manifest changes.

### The sticker object

```json
{
    "packId": "utyaduck",
    "id": "AgAD8gADVp29Cg",
    "emoji": ["😂"],
    "format": "tgs",
    "width": 512,
    "height": 512,
    "size": 5272,
    "sha256": "a34d668493927d74a2b2032a496f7e31f77d47c2e6f3a4e568933950d293e206"
}
```

- `id` — 1–128 characters from `A–Z`, `a–z`, `0–9`, `_`, and `-`, unique within its pack.
- `emoji` — the emoji the sticker stands for, possibly none; search and the category chips match
  them.
- `format` — `"webp"` for a still image, `"tgs"` for a gzip-compressed Lottie animation, or
  `"webm"` for a VP9 video with transparency. Animated stickers loop.
- `width`, `height` — its size in pixels, at most 512 on the longer side.
- `size`, `sha256` — its file's length and digest.

### `GET /v0/stickers/packs`

Every pack in the folder, in catalog order. Response — `200`:
`{ "packs": [ /* pack objects */ ] }`.

### `GET /v0/stickers/packs/:packId`

Response — `200`: `{ "pack": { ... }, "stickers": [ /* sticker objects, in pack order */ ] }`.
`404` for an unknown pack.

### `GET /v0/stickers/packs/:packId/stickers/:stickerId/file`

The sticker's file, with the same caching, `ETag`, `nosniff`, and sandboxing headers as frame
media. `Content-Type` is `image/webp`, `application/x-tgsticker`, or `video/webm`. With
`?as=lottie`, a `tgs` sticker is served as its uncompressed Lottie JSON, `application/json`; the
daemon decompresses it on the first request and keeps the result in its cache. Other formats ignore
`as`. `404` for an unknown pack or sticker.

### `GET /v0/stickers/search`

Query parameters: `q` (optional, 1–64 characters), `emoji` (optional, one emoji), `kind`
(optional, `stickers` or `emoji`, default `stickers`), and `limit` (optional, `1`–`200`, default
`100`). One of `q` and `emoji` is required.

Response — `200`: `{ "stickers": [ /* sticker objects */ ] }`. `emoji`, or a `q` that is one emoji,
matches stickers tagged with that emoji, ignoring skin tone and variation selectors. Any other `q`
matches the stickers of packs whose title has a word starting with it, ignoring case. Results come
in catalog order, the person's recents first.

### `GET /v0/stickers/recents`

Response — `200`:
`{ "recents": [{ "packId": "utyaduck", "stickerId": "AgAD8gADVp29Cg" }], "cursor": "01991f3a-..." }`.

At most 20 stickers the person used, most recent first. Posting a story records every sticker on
it. A sticker whose pack is no longer in the folder stays in the list; clients leave it out.
Follow `sticker_recents.updated` from `cursor`. Recents belong to each person: the authenticated
member in team mode, and the installation's one person in standalone mode.

### `POST /v0/stickers/recents`

`{ "packId": "...", "stickerId": "...", "mutationId": "..." }` moves the sticker to the front of
the caller's recents, dropping the oldest beyond 20. An unknown sticker is `404`. Response — `200`
with the list as from `GET`.

### `DELETE /v0/stickers/recents`

Clears the caller's recents, for the ✕ beside Recently used. The body is `{}` or
`{ "mutationId": "..." }`. Response — `200` with the empty list.

Every change emits `sticker_recents.updated` to that person's connections only, carrying
`recents` and optional `mutationId`.

## Live voice sessions

GPT-Live controls the initiating desktop window through a fixed set of UI-equivalent actions.
It is not bound to a coding agent and does not receive that agent's tools. The desktop verifies
each action's target and permissions and executes it through its existing UI/state actions. Only
public conversation text is shared: no coding tools, screen capture, other sessions' audio,
private reasoning, or tool logs. Voice covers only the agents of the daemon serving the call;
remote connections and peers are never read or acted on.

**Voice.** GPT-Live hands every request to the person's Voice agent: one long-running subtask of
Happy per person, created the first time that person starts a call and kept across calls. Voice is
an ordinary agent made the same way as Prioritizing (see Spaces): it appears in Happy's `subtasks`,
is created unreported, has a conversation of its own with its own row in the sidebar, and is
recognised by an agent ID the daemon stores for the person, not by metadata. When the person
archives it, the next call creates a new Voice. Voice has a fixed tool array and fixed
instructions that are not configurable: read-only sidebar tools, a tool that reads the public
user and assistant text of any conversation on this daemon the person can see, a request to
Prioritizing for any rearrangement, and every desktop action below for the window of the call it
is serving, so it can do whatever the person does by clicking. It never edits the sidebar itself.
Calls in several windows share the person's one Voice and one queue; each request's actions go
only to the window of the call that made it, and a request the person writes in Voice's
conversation has no window to act on.

Voice runs the machine's voice settings (`config.voice`, see `GET /v0/config`).
By default it uses `openai/gpt-6.1-sol` at low effort through the daemon's configured-default
provider or account pool. The settings may instead name any configured provider, account, or
account pool, any model that provider offers from any vendor, an effort that model accepts, and
fast mode. Fast mode requests the `priority` service tier only when the selected route's catalog
offers it; otherwise it is ignored, and the effective settings say so. The daemon captures the
selection when the call starts, verifies that the provider is enabled and offers the model and
effort, resolves one compatible enabled account, and freezes provider, account, model, effort,
and service tier for the call: every request of that call reaches Voice with that exact selection.
For an account pool it prefers the call's own voice credential account when the pool includes
it, then an account without a recent sign-in or usage failure. Later settings changes affect only
new calls. The explicitly selected voice credential remains separate. A creation request cannot
override this selection. An unavailable or incompatible controller route fails with
`503 live_unavailable` and a setup explanation; the call never silently switches models or
accounts. A selection whose model is incompatible with Voice's previous one starts Voice's
conversation afresh, as any agent's model change does.

The voice model's system prompt is the settings' override when one is stored, otherwise the
built-in default. It is also captured when the call starts.

Provider delegations enter a bounded first-in, first-out queue per person; an ordinary follow-up
is queued rather than refused as busy. Voice handles one request at a time: the next request is
given to it only after the previous one's outcome is delivered, and the desktop has at most one
outstanding action. Each request retains its original delegation identity and source transcript
identities. It reaches Voice as one message in Voice's conversation holding the delegation text,
the call's new attributed user and assistant transcript fragments, and the sidebar outline when it
changed since Voice last received one. That message is attributed provider context with agent
origin, never a person's own message, so it is not human authorization. Every dispatched desktop
action uses the current accepted context revision. Spoken outcomes use human-readable names rather
than internal IDs; ambiguous names require clarification.

Voice's conversation is durable and persists across calls like any agent's. It holds Voice's own
tool calls and results; ordinary automatic compaction bounds it. Coding tool calls, tool results,
private reasoning, and provider metadata of other conversations are never imported, and Voice's
own tool traffic is never copied into GPT-Live. The person may open Voice's conversation and
write to it; such a message is the person's own request, and Voice answers in text, not speech.
Completed actions remain recorded in the call's bounded deduplication ledger. Failed requests
report truthful plain-text outcomes and uncertainty and never replay a desktop action. A request
is never retried after it fails. Desktop actions are not durable tool calls: one interrupted by a
restart becomes an error result and is never executed again.

Each active request is bounded to 120 seconds, excluding queue time, and eight inference rounds;
at either limit the daemon stops Voice's turn and delivers a fixed explanation. Queued requests
expire after 120 seconds without starting an action. Closing voice cancels queued requests and
stops Voice's active turn; Voice itself remains. Already-started desktop mutations retain the
existing uncertainty and deduplication rules. When the frozen account's sign-in is rejected or its
usage runs out, the explanation says so and what the person can do.

A request whose actions all succeed and end in a terminal action — `desktopOpen`, `sessionSend`
staging, `composerDraftAppend`, `sessionModelChange`, or `taskCreate` — receives a fixed,
truthful outcome composed by the daemon from the verified results, delivered as soon as the last
result is verified. Voice's turn may continue to completion, but nothing more from that turn is
spoken. Requests that read state or receive a refusal or failure are answered with Voice's final
text for that request. No outcome is delivered to the voice model before the action it describes
has a verified result.

The voice model receives the person's sidebar outline as Markdown under a `# Sidebar` heading,
described below, when the call starts and again only when the sidebar itself changes. Agent
activity is never pushed: no run starts or settlements, message briefs, conversation statuses,
or desktop AppState. The outline is attributed data, not instructions, authorization, or an order
to speak. The voice model may answer directly from it; anything else, including whether some work
finished, requires a request to Voice, which looks it up when asked.

Every route uses ordinary API authentication. A session belongs to its creating principal;
another principal receives `404` for its ID and never receives its journal events. The session
is also bound to the initiating `windowId`; its control socket must name that same window.
The trusted desktop host binds the socket to the actual window, not a renderer-selected foreign
window. Provider transcripts and generated action text remain attributed provider context, not
automatically trusted human authorization. Existing permissions, role checks, and required human
confirmations still apply. Voice cannot answer permission prompts or grant bot administration.

The browser owns microphone and speaker WebRTC tracks. The daemon exchanges SDP using the
explicitly selected server-held credential and owns the sideband, delegation identities,
bounded text updates, and shutdown. Credentials never enter responses, events, URLs, or the
browser. Selecting a Codex subscription credential is an explicit experimental choice, not a
promise of entitlement; selecting an OpenAI API key explicitly opts into API billing. The
daemon never falls back between credential kinds, accounts, or transports after a refusal.
Use current GPT-Live only: the public `gpt-live-1` transport or Codex's native
`gpt-live-1-codex` integration. Never fall back to older Realtime models or protocols.

### The live session object

```json
{
    "id": "l1a2b3c4",
    "windowId": "desktop-window-1",
    "credential": { "type": "codex_subscription", "providerId": "codex" },
    "contextRevision": 1,
    "status": "active",
    "usage": { "seconds": 12, "final": false },
    "error": null,
    "createdAt": 1755400000000,
    "updatedAt": 1755400012000,
    "endedAt": null,
    "version": "01991f3a-6d2f-7000-8000-3a0b2c4d5e6f"
}
```

- `id` — Happy's CUID2 resource ID, distinct from any provider session or delegation ID.
- `windowId` — the initiating desktop window's opaque lifetime identity; immutable.
- `credential` — the immutable selection: `type` is `codex_subscription` or `openai_api_key`,
  and `providerId` names an enabled configured provider holding that exact credential kind.
  An unavailable provider, missing credential, or kind mismatch fails without trying another.
- `contextRevision` — the latest accepted desktop context revision, a positive safe integer.
  Context contents and watch selection remain private to the control connection, not the journal.
- `status` — `starting`, `active`, `closing`, `closed`, or `failed`. `active` requires a
  verified provider session and attached sideband. `closed` means an orderly deliberate end
  completed; it does not claim final usage. For public GPT-Live, this requires a valid
  `session.closed` with reason `close_requested` after our close request. Other provider close
  reasons or an unrequested terminal event are `failed`, even when final usage is confirmed.
  Unexpected transport loss, a transport error, an interrupted start, or an applicable close
  deadline instead means `failed`. Only `usage.final` reports confirmed final usage.
- `usage.seconds` — the latest cumulative voice duration, or `null` before the provider reports
  it. Never sum cumulative snapshots or infer billable duration from a local clock.
- `usage.final` — `true` only after the provider confirms final usage. It remains `false` on
  an interrupted connection without that confirmation. Coding-agent token usage remains on
  the ordinary agent usage surface; no token usage is invented from voice duration.
- `error` — a human-readable, sanitized explanation on failure, otherwise `null`; never a raw
  provider diagnostic, credential, or private transcript.
- `createdAt`, `updatedAt`, `endedAt` — timestamps; `endedAt` is `null` until local termination.
- `version` — the ordinary resource version for reads and event chaining.

### `POST /v0/live/sessions`

Creates one voice session. Body:

```json
{
    "mutationId": "start-voice-1",
    "id": "l1a2b3c4",
    "windowId": "desktop-window-1",
    "sdp": "<WebRTC SDP offer>",
    "credential": { "type": "codex_subscription", "providerId": "codex" },
    "contextRevision": 1,
    "context": {
        "windowId": "desktop-window-1",
        "connections": [],
        "activeConnectionId": null,
        "activeTarget": null,
        "projects": [],
        "workspaces": [],
        "sessions": [],
        "bots": [],
        "activeSession": null,
        "truncated": false
    }
}
```

`mutationId` and `id` are optional; other fields are required. The SDP is
nonblank and at most 65,536 characters. `providerId` is nonblank and at most 128 characters.
Raw credentials, arbitrary endpoints, model overrides, and permission overrides are not accepted.
Creation, credential-selection, context/control, and close request objects reject unknown fields.
The Live resource ID uses ordinary CUID2 syntax (2–32 lowercase alphanumeric characters, starting
with a letter). Desktop window and target IDs are opaque, nonblank strings of at most 128
characters without control characters; never derive one ID from another. The context window ID
must equal the outer window ID. The entire creation body is limited to 640 KiB.
The daemon chooses the supported Live model and transport for the selected credential kind.

Two optional fields negotiate additive desktop capabilities:

- `desktopCapabilities` — the capability names this desktop implements: unique strings of 1–64
  characters, at most 16. Send it only to a daemon whose `GET /v0/health` lists
  `capabilities.desktopLiveCapabilities`. The daemon accepts the intersection of this list and its
  own; unknown names are ignored, never rejected. Current names are `appState`, `voiceFocus`,
  `taskCreate`, `sessionModelChange`, and `agentTargets`. `taskCreate`, `sessionModelChange`, and
  `agentTargets` are accepted only together with `appState`. Omitted, the call uses only the
  original contract.
- `appState` — the initial Desktop AppState object described below. It is valid only when
  `desktopCapabilities` includes `appState`, and its serialized form is at most 256 KiB.
- `localConnectionId` — the desktop's connection ID for the daemon serving this request, an
  opaque ID as above. It is valid only when `desktopCapabilities` includes `agentTargets`, and is
  then required; the daemon accepts `agentTargets` only when it is present. Because creation
  rejects unknown fields, send it only to a daemon whose `capabilities.desktopLiveCapabilities`
  lists `agentTargets`. The daemon acts only on this connection and refuses any target on another.

The accepted capability list is echoed in control `hello`. A desktop must not send fields,
frames, task targets, or action results that belong to a capability the daemon did not accept,
and the daemon never sends an action or frame for a capability the desktop did not request.

Response — `201`:

```json
{
    "session": { "id": "l1a2b3c4", "windowId": "desktop-window-1", "status": "starting" },
    "transport": { "type": "webrtc", "sdp": "<WebRTC SDP answer>" }
}
```

`session` is the complete live session object. Apply the answer to the browser peer connection
and wait for identity-checked control `hello` followed by `status: active` before sending
application commands. The renderer need not interpret raw provider readiness events. The create request already starts
the call; do not send another start event. Only this response includes SDP, never GET or events.
The response returns after upstream call allocation and sideband attachment; it must not wait
for browser media negotiation. `starting` transitions asynchronously to `active` when the
provider confirms readiness after the browser applies the answer. An already-ready call may
instead return `active`.

Readiness requires an observed, valid provider `session.started` or dialect-supported
`session.updated` on the attached sideband. HTTP success, a socket opening, ICE negotiation,
and the browser data channel are not readiness evidence. The native Codex adapter may send
one native `session.update` with the already-selected instructions/settings to obtain that
confirmation; rejection or lack of confirmation fails the call without retry. The public
adapter must not send this native update or change immutable startup settings. If no valid
confirmation arrives within the readiness deadline, fail with a clear startup explanation.

The daemon reserves the local resource before the single upstream create attempt. Reusing its
ID returns `409 conflict` with the current `session`, including after an uncertain outcome; it
never creates another upstream call. `mutationId` remains an echo, not a deduplication key.
Clients should supply an ID, read it after a lost response, and close it before deliberately
creating a replacement. An unknown upstream outcome is reported as failure, not retried.

Invalid requests are `400 invalid_request`, a provider
credential refusal is `403 forbidden`, and an unsupported credential transport is `501 unsupported`.
An unavailable credential/provider or failed upstream connection is `503 live_unavailable`.
Errors after reservation additionally carry the current `session`; errors before reservation
create no resource or events. Call allocation and sideband attachment have a 30-second deadline;
provider readiness after returning the answer has a separate 30-second deadline. There may be at most four
nonterminal sessions per principal and at most one per window; further creates return
`409 conflict` without an upstream call. The desktop must attach its control connection within
15 seconds of creation; failure to attach ends the voice session without starting actions.

### `GET /v0/live/sessions/:id`

Response — `200`: `{ "session": { ... } }`. Returns the complete current resource, including
terminal state and whether usage finalized. `404` for an unknown ID or another principal's session.

### `POST /v0/live/sessions/:id/close`

Body: `{ "mutationId": "stop-voice-1" }`, with `mutationId` optional.

Response — `200`: `{ "session": { ... } }`. Records a close request and may return `closing`;
that state does not claim provider finalization. Repeating close, including on a terminal session,
is harmless. Unknown or another principal's IDs return `404`. No `If-Match` is required.

Stop accepting delegations and send the selected transport's `session.close` once. Public
GPT-Live keeps the sideband alive for up to 15 seconds for the documented `session.closed`
event and final usage. A valid event with reason `close_requested` after our close request is
`closed`; other reasons or an unrequested terminal event are `failed` with confirmed final
usage when supplied. Timeout or premature transport loss is `failed` with usage unconfirmed.
Native Codex sends its close command and closes the transport, following its native lifecycle.
An orderly deliberate native end settles as `closed`, with the last reported duration or null
and `usage.final: false` unless actual final provider usage was received. A local disconnect or
normal WebSocket close never invents a provider event or final usage. A transport error or
interrupted start remains `failed`. Closing voice does not abort, archive, or recreate any coding
session or its tasks.
Daemon shutdown closes owned calls; restart marks unfinished local sessions failed and never
replays creation or delegated mutations. A Voice turn a restart interrupted is stopped, not
resumed, and its desktop actions are never executed again. Terminal records remain readable for seven days,
bounded to the newest 1,000 per principal. Evicted records return `404`.

### Desktop context and targets

The initial `context` and subsequent `desktopContext` frames carry the same closed object:

- `windowId` — the initiating window identity.
- `connections` — up to 100 `{ connectionId, name, online }` entries.
- `activeConnectionId` — an explicitly selected connection ID, or `null`.
- `activeTarget` — one desktop target below, or `null`.
- `projects` — up to 100 `{ target: ProjectTarget, name }` entries.
- `workspaces` — up to 100 `{ target: WorkspaceTarget, name, status }` entries; workspace
  status is `ready`, `preparing`, `error`, or `unknown`, not a conversation activity state.
- `sessions` — up to 100 `{ target: SessionRef, title, status }` entries; title may be `null`.
- `bots` — up to 100 `{ target: BotTarget, name, status }` entries.
- `activeSession` — `null` or `{ target: SessionRef, status, messages, composerHasDraft,
writeRefusal }`. `writeRefusal` is a bounded human-readable reason or `null`.
- `truncated` — whether the bounded projection omitted visible entities or text.

Names and titles are at most 256 characters. `writeRefusal` is at most 1,024 characters.
Conversation status is exactly `idle`, `running`, `awaitingInput`, `waiting`, `error`, or
`unknown`; it comes from structured application state, never text matching. `messages` contains
at most 50 `{ id, role, text }` public messages with `role` either `user` or `assistant` and
`text` at most 16,384 characters. Exclude private reasoning, tool arguments/results, attachments,
files, credentials, and composer draft contents. The draft is only a boolean in context.

References are namespaced; IDs from different connections never identify the same entity:

- `ProjectRef`: `{ connectionId, projectId }`.
- `GroupRef`: `{ connectionId, groupId }`.
- `SessionRef`: `{ connectionId, groupId, sessionId }`.
- `ProjectTarget`: `{ kind: "project", connectionId, projectId, groupId }`.
- `WorkspaceTarget`: `{ kind: "workspace", connectionId, projectId, workspaceId, groupId }`.
- `SessionTarget`: `{ kind: "session", connectionId, groupId, sessionId }`.
- `BotTarget`: `{ kind: "bot", connectionId, groupId, sessionId, botId }`.
- `TaskTarget`: `{ kind: "task", connectionId, groupId, sessionId, taskId }` — `groupId` is the
  task's workspace and `sessionId` its one agent.

A `DesktopTarget` is exactly one of those target variants. `TaskTarget` may appear only in a call
whose accepted capabilities include `appState`; older daemons reject it. Group IDs are supplied
explicitly even where they currently coincide with project or workspace IDs. The desktop maps
its internal worktree identity to wire `workspaceId` explicitly, never by parsing names or paths.
A task's or bot's conversation is an ordinary `SessionRef` for the session actions, and the
desktop lists task conversations in `sessions`.

With the `agentTargets` capability, an action may also name a conversation by this daemon's own
agent ID, as Voice reads it from the sidebar and conversation tools:

- `AgentTarget`: `{ kind: "agent", connectionId, agentId }` — the conversation of agent `agentId`
  on connection `connectionId`, which is always the call's `localConnectionId`. `agentId` is the
  daemon's agent ID, at most 128 characters.

An `AgentTarget` may appear wherever an action takes a `DesktopTarget` or a `SessionRef`. The
desktop resolves it to its own entity for that agent — a task's, a bot's, a subtask's, or another
conversation's — and refuses an agent it cannot show with `notFound`. A result whose `target`
answers an action that named an `AgentTarget` may name either form. Without `agentTargets` the
daemon never sends an `AgentTarget`, and older desktops are unaffected.

### Desktop AppState

With the `appState` capability, the desktop also supplies an AppState object: the whole
sidebar the person sees, already filtered and in canonical sidebar order, plus the focused
conversation. It is a closed object:

- `timeZone` — the desktop's IANA time zone, such as `America/Los_Angeles`, at most 64 characters.
- `servers` — up to 32 `{ connectionId, name, kind, online, catalog, bots, tasks, projects,
models }` entries in sidebar order: the local server first, then remote connections.
  `kind` is `local` or `remote`; `catalog` is `ready`, `loading`, or `error`. - `bots` — up to 100 `{ target: BotTarget, name, status, lastActivityAt }` top-level bots. - `tasks` — up to 100 `{ target: TaskTarget, name, status, lastActivityAt }` top-level tasks
  in the person's own order. - `projects` — up to 100 `{ target: ProjectTarget, name, sessions, workspaces }`, where
  `workspaces` holds up to 100 `{ target: WorkspaceTarget, name, status, sessions }` and each
  `sessions` holds up to 100 `{ target: SessionRef, title, status, lastActivityAt }` direct
  conversations of that project root or workspace, in tab order. - `models` — up to 200 `{ providerId, providerName, modelId, name, efforts, defaultEffort }`
  entries: the model choices this server's composer picker offers. `providerName` is the
  picker's human-readable service label.
- `focused` — `null` or `{ target: DesktopTarget, title, latestMessage }`. `latestMessage` is
  `{ state: "loaded", role, text, truncated }` for the latest loaded, settled public user or
  assistant message; `{ state: "empty" }` when the loaded conversation has none; or
  `{ state: "notLoaded" }` when its transcript is not loaded.
- `truncated` — whether the desktop omitted entities or text to stay within bounds.

Bots and tasks are top-level only. Bot and task descendants, subtasks, and workspaces owned by
them never appear. `status` is the conversation status above; workspace `status` is the workspace
status above. `lastActivityAt` is epoch milliseconds floored to the minute, or `null`. Names and
titles are at most 256 characters; message text is at most 16,384 characters; efforts and IDs are
at most 128 characters. The serialized object is at most 256 KiB.

The desktop sends a complete replacement as the creation request's `appState` and later as the
`appState` control frame. It is not revisioned, persisted, or journaled; the latest frame wins and
identical repeats are harmless. AppState changes never advance `desktopContext` revisions, and
background AppState never stales an action. The desktop coalesces changes before sending them and
sends only settled message text, never streaming partial text. With the `agentTargets`
capability the desktop sends the `appState` frame only when a server's `models` change, never for
activity, status, names, order, focus, or messages.

The daemon uses AppState only to resolve names and targets and to validate model choices for
Voice's actions. It never pushes AppState, or anything derived from it, to the voice model.

### Sidebar outline

The voice model receives the person's own sidebar on this daemon, never the desktop's: the rows
of the sidebar document in its order (see Spaces), each section's label, and the sessions the
document shows under a row. A person without a sidebar document gets Happy, their bots, their
tasks, and their joined Surfaces in the classic order. The outline holds names only: no
conversation status, activity times, messages, or IDs. Folded sections and rows are named once,
with the number of rows they hold:

```markdown
# Sidebar

- Happy
- Prioritizing

## Releases

- Preview viewer release
    - Release notes

## Distractions (folded, 6 rows)
```

The outline is bounded to 8 KiB and marks omitted entries explicitly; omitted entries are never
sent later. It is sent as the call's initial context and again, as a complete replacement under
the same heading, only when the outline's text changes, such as a new sidebar version, a renamed
row, or new or archived work. A changed outline waits while a voice request lacks its delivered
outcome and is sent after it; several changes meanwhile send one outline. Nothing else is pushed
to the voice model in the background.

### `GET /v0/live/sessions/:id/control?windowId=...`

Upgrades to a full-duplex WebSocket using ordinary Happy API bearer authentication. The trusted
desktop host attaches authentication; never put a bearer token in the URL or renderer state.
`windowId` is required and must match the resource. Unknown/foreign resources are `404`, a
mismatched window or an already attached controller is `409 conflict`, and a terminal resource
cannot attach. There is only one controller per call. Frames are UTF-8 JSON, at most 256 KiB.
Invalid/oversized frames fail the connection; raw audio and raw provider frames are not accepted.
The whole serialized context must fit the frame budget, even when each individual field fits
its own limit. Omit older/less relevant text and entities and set `truncated` rather than
assuming every per-field maximum can fit simultaneously.

The server first sends `{ type: "hello", sessionId, windowId, contextRevision, capabilities? }`.
The desktop verifies all three identities before acting. `capabilities` is present only when the
creation request carried `desktopCapabilities`, and lists the accepted capability names.
Client-to-server frames are:

- `{ type: "desktopContext", revision, context }` — a complete replacement, with strictly
  increasing positive safe-integer `revision`. Exact same-revision duplicates are harmless;
  older revisions are ignored; different contents at the same revision fail the connection.
  Focus, catalog, access, and draft-presence changes advance the revision. Public text streaming
  does not advance it token by token.
